Gradient catastrophes and an infinite hierarchy of Hölder cusp-singularities for 1D Euler
Isaac Neal, Steve Shkoller, Vlad Vicol

Abstract
. We establish an infinite hierarchy of finite-time gradient catastrophes for smooth solutions of the 1D Euler equations of gas dynamics with non-constant entropy. Specifically, for all integers n ≥ 1 n\geq 1 , we prove that there exist classical solutions, emanating from smooth, compressive, and non-vacuous initial data, which form a cusp-type gradient singularity in finite time, in which the gradient of the solution has precisely C 0 , 1 2 ​ n + 1 C^{0,\frac{1}{2n+1}} Hölder-regularity. We show that such Euler solutions are codimension- ( 2 ​ n − 2 ) (2n-2) stable in the Sobolev space W 2 ​ n + 2 , ∞ W^{2n+2,\infty} .



1.
Introduction

One of the central problems in the mathematical theory of the compressible Euler equations, the canonical macroscopic model of gas dynamics, is the
formation
of gradient catastrophes from
smooth initial data
. The archetypal
singular solution
contains a
shock
, a codimension-1 subset of spacetime across which the solution experiences jump discontinuities. Compression in the initial conditions ensures that smooth sound waves steepen
further and further, until an infinite gradient is formed along a spacetime set of “first gradient singularities”, which we call the
pre-shock
. For localized one-dimensional initial data, the pre-shock is a point in spacetime, along which the gradient of the Euler solution forms a Hölder-type cusp singularity, in which the state variables remain Hölder continuous, but with gradients that blow up. It is only
after the pre-shock
, instantaneously, that the state variables become discontinuous, resulting in an entropy-producing shock wave. As such, a detailed analysis of the formation of the gradient singularity at the pre-shock is crucial to our understanding of the transition from smooth to discontinuous solutions of the Euler system.

The goal of this paper is to prove that the one-dimensional Euler dynamics, starting from from smooth and non-vacuous initial data, can attain an infinite hierarchy of finite-time cusp-type singularities at the pre-shock,
with a Hölder exponent that is indexed by an integer
n\geq 1
. By a slight abuse of terminology, we shall refer to the Euler solution at the time of the
first gradient blowup as the
pre-shock solution
or simply as the
pre-shock
.
Then, the
n^{\rm th}
pre-shock solution in this hierarchy corresponds to a cusp-singularity with Hölder exponent
\nicefrac{{1}}{{(2n+1)}}
. We analyze the stability of these singular pre-shock solutions and establish that the set of initial conditions leading to a
C^{0,\frac{1}{2n+1}}
cusp forms a
(2n-2)
-dimensional Banach submanifold of the Sobolev space
W^{2n+2,\infty}
; in particular, for all
n\geq 2
, only finite codimension stability holds.

The case
n=1
, corresponding to the fully-stable
C^{0,\frac{1}{3}}
pre-shock, has been fully investigated in 1D and under symmetry in
[
30
,
18
,
27
,
44
,
9
,
6
,
39
]
, and in multiple space dimensions in
[
10
,
11
,
42
]
.
The
only
prior result concerning the formation of an unstable pre-shock was obtained in
[
8
]
, which treats the special case
n=2
in the setting of azimuthal symmetry, using a modulated stability analysis of an explicit self-similar blowup profile.

Herein, we simultaneously analyze all
C^{0,\frac{1}{2n+1}}
pre-shock solutions in the cusp hierarchy (meaning, all
n\geq 1
) without making reference to an self-similar blowup profiles. We achieve this by taking a
purely characteristic perspective
, in which the pre-shock singularity is characterized by the
narrowing distance between the nearby fast-acoustic characteristics. In this Lagrangian-like characteristic framework, we show that carefully designed
differentiated Riemann variables
remain uniformly smooth up to the time of the first gradient singularity. In turn, this allows us to apply the Implicit Function Theorem in a smooth setting, to precisely characterize the finite-codimension stability. Our method of analysis for stable and unstable shock formation is sufficiently robust so as
to easily generalize to more complicated hyperbolic systems of conservation laws.

1.1.
The 1D Euler equations

The one-dimensional Euler equations are given as the system of conservation laws

(1.1a)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\rho+\partial_{y}(\rho u)

\displaystyle=0\,,

(1.1b)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}(\rho u)+\partial_{y}(\rho u^{2}+p)

\displaystyle=0\,,

(1.1c)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}E+\partial_{y}((p+E)u)

\displaystyle=0\,,

where the unknowns
u,\rho,E,
and
p
are scalar functions defined for
(y,t)\in\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
. Here
u
is the fluid velocity,
\rho
is the (strictly positive) density,
E
is the specific total energy, and
p
is the pressure. To close the system, one introduces an equation of state that relates the internal energy
E-\tfrac{1}{2}\rho u^{2}
to
p
and
\rho
. For an ideal gas, the equation of state is

(1.2)

p=(\gamma-1)(E-\tfrac{1}{2}\rho u^{2}),

where
\gamma>1
is a fixed adiabatic exponent.

For the study of shock formation, it is convenient to express the internal energy and pressure as functions of
\rho
and the specific entropy
S
, and to
express the Euler evolution in terms of the variables
(u,\rho,S)
. For ideal gases the specific entropy
S
is defined by the relation

(1.3)

p=\tfrac{1}{\gamma}\rho^{\gamma}e^{S}.

Introducing the rescaled adiabtic exponent
\alpha:=\tfrac{\gamma-1}{2}
and defining the rescaled sound speed
1
1

1

The actual sound speed in a compressible fluid is
c=\sqrt{\nicefrac{{\partial p}}{{\partial\rho}}}=e^{\nicefrac{{S}}{{2}}}\rho^{\alpha}=\alpha\sigma
.
by

(1.4)

\sigma=\tfrac{1}{\alpha}\sqrt{\tfrac{\partial p}{\partial\rho}}=\tfrac{1}{\alpha}e^{\nicefrac{{S}}{{2}}}\rho^{\alpha}\,,

we can rewrite the one-dimensional Euler equations (
1.1
)–(
1.2
) in terms of the variables
(u,\sigma,S)
as

(1.5a)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\sigma+u\partial_{y}\sigma+\alpha\sigma\partial_{y}u

\displaystyle=0\,,

(1.5b)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u+u\partial_{y}u+\alpha\sigma\partial_{y}\sigma

\displaystyle=\tfrac{\alpha}{2\gamma}\sigma^{2}\partial_{y}S\,,

(1.5c)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}S+u\partial_{y}S

\displaystyle=0\,.

Prior to the development of a discontintuous shock wave, the systems (
1.1
)–(
1.2
) and (
1.3
)–(
1.5
) are equivalent.

1.2.
Prior results

The theory of shock wave solutions for systems of hyperbolic conservation laws in
one space dimension
is well-developed. For a detailed exposition we refer the reader to
[
24
,
28
,
15
,
25
,
32
]
and the references therein. Herein, we shall only summarize results concerning the finite-time formation of gradient singularities for the Euler equations, from smooth initial conditions.

The fact that smooth Euler solutions generically have a finite lifespan is well-known
[
41
,
29
,
26
,
31
,
35
,
43
]
; such results rely upon a
proof by contradiction
argument to show that a finite-time breakdown of the smooth solution occurs.
While such arguments apply in great generality, they generally do not provide detailed information about the emerging gradient catastrophe. We emphasize that a precise characterization of the first gradient singularity is crucial for solving the physical shock development problem (see, for example, the discussion in
[
7
]
).

Recently, a number of
constructive singularity formation
results have
been established for the Euler equations. Constructive results show that for an appropriate class of smooth initial data, the
very first
gradient singularity can be precisely described, either as being of
shock-type
2
2

2

For shocks the gradients of the fundamental variables (such as velocity and density) blow up, while the fundamental variables themselves remain bounded, and even retain limited Hölder regularity, which characterizes the type of cusp that forms.
or as being an
implosion
3
3

3

For implosions the fundamental variables (such as velocity and density) blow up themselves, along with their gradients.
, and that no other singularity can occur prior. Since in one space dimension neither implosion
4
4

4

Smooth solutions that implode at the very first singularity have been recently constructed rigorously in
multiple space dimensions
, for the isentropic problem in radial symmetry
[
36
,
37
,
5
]
. These results have been extended perturbatively to hold also outside of symmetry constraints
[
12
]
, and they have been used to establish the finite time blowup of vorticity
[
17
,
16
]
for the multi-dimensional Euler system. We note however that all known smooth implosions have only been proven to be finite codimensions stable, and that the dimension of the instability remains unquantified.

nor vacuum formation is possible as a first singularity from smooth solutions
[
14
,
13
]
, we henceforth focus exclusively on constructive proofs of shock-type singularities. More precisely, when the fundamental variables
(u,\sigma,S)
remain Hölder continuous at the time of the first gradient catastrophe, we refer to the emerging singularity as a
pre-shock
.

With the exception of the recent papers
[
8
]
and
[
38
]
, all existing constructive blowup results for the Euler system establish the formation of a
C^{0,\frac{1}{3}}
Hölder pre-shock as the first singularity. This is because the
C^{0,\frac{1}{3}}
pre-shock emerges in a
stable
fashion from a large open set of smooth and generic initial data. A compendium is listed as follows:

•

The paper
[
30
]
established both shock formation (as a
C^{0,\frac{1}{3}}
pre-shock) and shock development for the one-dimensional
p
-system. Further refinements were obtained in
[
18
]
and
[
27
]
. For the three-dimensional Euler equations in spherical symmetry,
[
44
]
used similar ideas to construct solutions which form a
C^{0,\frac{1}{3}}
cusp (with respect to the radial coordinate) in finite time, and to show that the resulting weak entropy solution instantaneously develops a radial shock (jump discontinuity).

•

Results similar to
[
44
]
have been established in
[
21
]
, by using techniques originating in mathematical general relativity. More precisely,
[
21
]
appeals to part of the framework developed in
[
19
]
. The monograph
[
19
]
established shock formation for multi-dimensional irrotational flows (see also
[
22
]
), by studying the second order wave equation satisfied by the velocity potential. This framework was also used in
[
20
]
to analyze the
restricted
5
5

5

In the
restricted
shock development problem, the flow is artificially constrained to remain irrotational and isentropic even after the shock forms, which violates the second law of thermodynamics and the Rankine-Hugoniot jump conditions.
shock development problem for multi-dimensional irrotational and isentropic flows, and it was used in
[
33
,
34
]
to prove shock formation for the two- and three-dimensional Euler equations for initial data close to a plane wave, in the presence of vorticity and entropy. We emphasize that the shock formation results obtained in
[
19
,
22
,
21
,
20
,
33
,
34
]
do not provide the precise Hölder regularity of the Euler solution at the time of the very first singularity.

•

An alternative approach to shock formation for the Euler equations was proposed in
[
9
]
. This approach employs modulated self-similar analysis
6
6

6

Finite-time blowup results for
2
+
1
-dimensional second-order quasilinear wave equations, which do not satisfy the null-condition (akin to irrotational isentropic Euler), were previously obtained in
[
3
,
2
]
. The proofs in
[
3
,
2
]
use a “blowup technique” which resembles self-similar analysis, but without the modulation parameters corresponding to unstable modes arising from the Galilean symmetry of the equations.
to establish the stability of an explicit blowup profile. The paper
[
9
]
considers the two-dimensional Euler equations in azimuthal symmetry and establishes the stable formation of a
C^{0,\frac{1}{3}}
pre-shock (with respect to the angular variable) in finite time, from generic and smooth initial data. A more refined self-similar approach was then used in
[
10
,
11
]
to prove stable gradient singularity formation for the full three-dimensional Euler system, in the absence of any symmetry assumptions. The papers
[
10
,
11
]
establish the formation of a
stable point-shock
from smooth and generic initial data. Point-shock solutions are multi-dimensional analogues of the one-dimensional pre-shock
solutions; they occur about a distinguished point in space-time and quantify how the solution forms a
C^{0,\frac{1}{3}}
cusp singularity in one direction, while remaining smooth in the orthogonal directions. We emphasize that while proofs of shock formation based on self-similar analysis provide detailed
information about the solution at the point of the very first singularity (the point-shock mentioned earlier), these methods are ill-suited for resolving the physical shock-development problem.

•

In
[
39
]
we have revisited the results of
[
9
]
from a
characteristics-based
perspective, adapted to the three distinct wave-families present in the Euler equations. For the two-dimensional Euler equations in azimuthal symmetry, we proved the stable formation of
C^{0,\frac{1}{3}}
cusps in finite time, from smooth and generic initial conditions, in a more general setting than
[
9
]
. A three-wave-family characteristics-based approach, was also used in
[
6
]
to resolve the shock development problem for the two-dimensional Euler equations in azimuthal symmetry. By utilizing the
C^{0,\frac{1}{3}}
pre-shock as new Cauchy data for the Euler evolution, the paper
[
6
]
shows that simultaneously to the discontinuous shock, a weak-rarefaction and a weak-contact singularity emerge from the pre-shock.

•

Recently, pre-shock formation
past the time of the first singularity
has been established in the context of the maximal globally hyperbolic development (MGHD) of smooth and generic Cauchy data, for the multi-dimensional Euler equations. In
[
42
]
, the last two authors of this paper have constructed a fundamental piece of the boundary of the MGHD, which is necessary for the local shock development
problem. Specifically,
[
42
]
used a precise geometric analysis of fast-acoustic characteristic surfaces and a new type of differentiated Riemann variables to smoothly evolve the Euler solution up to the future temporal boundary of the MGHD, which is a singular hypersurface in spacetime containing the union of three sets: (i) a codimension-
2
set of first singularities all of which are
C^{0,\frac{1}{3}}
pre-shocks; (ii) a codimension-
1
hypersurface emanating from the pre-shock set in the downstream direction, on which the Euler solution experiences a continuum of gradient catastrophes; and (iii) a codimension-
1
Cauchy horizon emanating upstream from the pre-shock set, which the Euler solution cannot reach. We also mention the preprint
[
1
]
which evolves the Euler solution up to a portion of the boundary of the MGHD, containing only the sets in (i) and (ii) above, but not the Cauchy horizon. We note that the proof framework employed in this manuscript closely resembles the one in
[
42
]
, except that, in this case, we are not confronted with the geometric complexities inherent in multiple space dimensions.

To reiterate, every constructive singularity formation result cited in the list above demonstrates the formation of a stable
C^{0,\frac{1}{3}}
cusp as the initial gradient singularity of a smooth Euler evolution. So far, only two the papers
[
8
]
and
[
38
]
have established the formation of gradient singularity which is
not
a
C^{0,\frac{1}{3}}
cusp:

•

For the compressible Euler equations in one space dimension, in
[
38
]
we have exhibited an open set of initial data, such that the classical solution of (
1.5
) forms a
C^{0,\alpha}
pre-shock as a first singularity, for any
\alpha\in[\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}},1)
. Note that these pre-shock singularities correspond to Hölder exponents
strictly larger
than
\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}
. Moreover, their formation is stable with respect to small perturbations of the initial data in the topology of
C^{1,\frac{1-\alpha}{\alpha}-\delta}
, for any small
\delta>0
. We emphasize however that these exotic cusps (with Hölder exponent larger that
\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}
) do not arise from smooth initial conditions; in fact, such initial data cannot lie in
C^{2,0}
.

•

For smooth initial conditions, the only paper which constructed a non-
C^{0,\frac{1}{3}}
pre-shock for the Euler equations is
[
8
]
. The authors of
[
8
]
have used modulated self-similar stability analysis of an explicit blowup profile to construct solutions of the two-dimensional isentropic Euler equations in azimuthal symmetry, which form a
C^{0,\frac{1}{5}}
cusp as a first singularity, and have proven that such a solution is codimension-
2
stable with respect to
C^{8}
perturbations. The paper
[
8
]
builds on the self-similar analysis developed in
[
9
]
, and uses information about the explicit smooth unstable blowup profiles for the 1D Burgers equation (see also
[
23
]
and the discussion in §
2
below).
7
7

7

We mention here also the paper
[
40
]
, which does not treat the Euler system, but instead considers dispersive and dissipative perturbations of the Burgers equation such as fractional KdV, fractal Burgers, and the Whitham equation. Theorem 1.1 in
[
40
]
shows that for a codimension
2n-2
subset of
H^{2n+3}(\mathbb{T})
, the aforementioned models form a
C^{0,\frac{1}{2n+1}}
cusp as the first gradient singularity. As with
[
8
]
, the proof in
[
40
]
is based on modulation theory, where the well-known smooth self-similar solutions to the inviscid Burgers equation (see
[
23
]
) are used as “profiles” in weighted
L^{2}
-based stability estimates. The proof approach taken in our paper avoids self-similar analysis, or knowledge of any explicit blowup profiles.
The result in
[
8
]
corresponds to the
special case
n=2
and
S_{0}\equiv 0
in our main result
, Theorem
1.1
below. We emphasize that the proof presented in this manuscript is fundamentally different than the one in
[
8
]
: instead of modulated self-similar stability analysis, we appeal to the analysis of differentiated Riemann-type variables in the coordinates of the fast-acoustic characteristics. We believe that this perspective is more robust (as seen also in
[
38
,
42
]
), and generalizes in a straightforward way to general classes of hyperbolic systems of conservation laws.

1.3.
Main results

Our main result concerns the construction and the stability analysis of an infinite hierarchy of pre-shock solutions which arise as the first gradient singularity for velocity and sound speed in smooth one-dimensional Euler dynamics. The
n^{\rm th}
pre-shock in this hierarchy (
n\geq 1
is an integer) corresponds
to a
C^{0,\frac{1}{2n+1}}
cusp-singularity, and forms from initial data lying on a
(2n-2)
-dimensional Banach submanifold of
W^{2n+2,\infty}
. A precise statement of our main result is given in Theorem
9.1
below, while an abbreviated statement is given next in Theorem
1.1
.

Fix
n\geq 1
an integer. Let us introduce a function
\overline{w}_{0}\colon\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{R}
which has
2n+2
bounded derivatives, whose derivative
\overline{w}_{0}^{\prime}
attains a global minimum at a distinguished point in
\mathbb{T}
, and such that at this global minimum the function
\overline{w}_{0}^{\prime}
exhibits flatness of order
2n
, meaning that the functions
\{\partial_{y}^{j}\overline{w}_{0}^{\prime}\}_{j=1}^{2n-1}
all vanish here, but
\partial_{y}^{2n}\overline{w}_{0}^{\prime}
does not. Using the translation invariance of the Euler equations we may assume w.l.o.g. that
\overline{w}_{0}^{\prime}
attains its global minimum at
y=0
. Moreover, using the hyperbolic scaling invariance of the Euler equations (see, for example, Section 1.3 in
[
10
]
), we may assume w.l.o.g. that
\overline{w}_{0}^{\prime}(0)=-1
. The prototypical such function is given as

\overline{w}_{0}^{\prime}(y)=-1+y^{2n}\,,\qquad\mbox{for }y\mbox{ close to }0\,,

suitably smooth for
y
away from
0
,
\mathbb{T}
-periodic, and with zero mean on
\mathbb{T}
; this last condition ensures that the function
w_{0}(y)=w_{0}(0)+\int_{0}^{y}\overline{w}_{0}^{\prime}(\overline{y})d\overline{y}
is
\mathbb{T}
-periodic. The constant
w_{0}(0)
is chosen such that
w_{0}>0
uniformly on
\mathbb{T}
, which is a “no-vacuum” assumption on the initial data.
A precise definition of all permissible functions
\overline{w}_{0}
satisfying the above properties is given in (
8.1
) below. We henceforth fix such a function
\overline{w}_{0}
.

Theorem 1.1

(
Finite codimension-stable shock formation, abbreviated).

Let
n\geq 1
be an integer, and fix the adiabatic exponent
\gamma>1
.
Then there exists a codimension
(2n-2)
Banach submanifold
\mathcal{M}_{n}
of
(W^{2n+2,\infty}(\mathbb{T}))^{3}
which contains the point
(\overline{u}_{0},\overline{\sigma}_{0},\overline{S}_{0}):=\frac{1}{2}(\overline{w}_{0},\overline{w}_{0},0)
, and an
\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}(n,\gamma)>0
such that for all
0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
the following holds. For any initial data
(u_{0},\sigma_{0},S_{0})\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\cap B_{\varepsilon}(\overline{u}_{0},\overline{\sigma}_{0},\overline{S}_{0})
(the ball of radius
\varepsilon
is taken with respect to the topology of
W^{2n+2,\infty}(\mathbb{T})
), the unique classical solution
(u,\sigma,S)
of the one-dimensional Euler equations (
1.5
) with initial data
(u,\sigma,S)|_{t=0}=(u_{0},\sigma_{0},S_{0})
forms a first gradient singularity for
u
and
\sigma
at time
T_{*}=\frac{2}{1+\alpha}+\mathcal{O}_{\gamma}(\varepsilon)
.
8
8

8

Here and throughout the paper, the notation
A\lesssim B
is used to say that there exists a constant
C>0
, which is independent of
\gamma,n,\varepsilon
, such that
A\leq CB
. We write
A=\mathcal{O}(B)
when
|A|\lesssim B
. If the implicit constant
C
in the
\lesssim
symbol also depends on a parameter
F
, we write
A\lesssim_{F}B
, and respectively
A=\mathcal{O}_{F}(B)
. See §
1.5
for details.
The function
S
remains
C^{1,\frac{1}{2n+1}}
smooth uniformly up to time
T_{*}
. Furthermore, at time
T_{*}
there exists a distinguished blowup point
y_{*}\in\mathbb{T}
, such that away from
y=y_{*}
, the variables
(u,\sigma,S)(\cdot,T_{*})
remain
W^{2n+2,\infty}
smooth. Finally, the gradient singularity experienced by the velocity and sound speed correspond to a
C^{0,\frac{1}{2n+1}}
pre-shock, and the corresponding cusp profile is given by

(1.6a)

\displaystyle u(y,T_{*})

\displaystyle=u(y_{*},T_{*})-\mathsf{b}\,(y-y_{*})^{\frac{1}{2n+1}}+\mathcal{O}_{\gamma,n}(|y-y_{*}|^{\frac{2}{2n+1}}),

(1.6b)

\displaystyle\sigma(y,T_{*})

\displaystyle=\sigma(y_{*},T_{*})-\mathsf{b}\,(y-y_{*})^{\frac{1}{2n+1}}+\mathcal{O}_{\gamma,n}(|y-y_{*}|^{\frac{2}{2n+1}}),

(1.6c)

\displaystyle S(y,T_{*})

\displaystyle=S(y_{*},T_{*})+\partial_{y}S(y_{*},T_{*})(y-y_{*})+\mathcal{O}_{\gamma,n}(\varepsilon|y-y_{*}|^{1+\frac{1}{2n+1}}),

for all
y
such that
|y-y_{*}|^{\frac{1}{2n+1}}\lesssim 1
and a constant
\mathsf{b}
with the asymptotic
\mathsf{b}=(2n+1)^{\frac{1}{2n+1}}+\mathcal{O}_{\gamma,n}(\varepsilon)
.

The precise statement of our main result is given in Theorem
9.1
below, where both the assumptions and the conclusions are stated in terms of the Riemann variables
(w,z,k):=(u+\sigma,u-\sigma,S)
defined in (
3.1
). At this stage, we make a few comments concerning the statement of Theorem
1.1
:

(i)

In the case
n=1
,
\mathcal{M}_{1}
is a codimension-
0
submanifold of
(W^{4,\infty}(\mathbb{T}))^{3}
containing the point
(\overline{u}_{0},\overline{\sigma}_{0},\overline{S}_{0})
. A different way to state the
codimension-
0
property of
\mathcal{M}_{1}
is to say that it is an
open subset
of
(W^{4,\infty}(\mathbb{T}))^{3}
containing
(\overline{u}_{0},\overline{\sigma}_{0},\overline{S}_{0})
. In this case the cusp structure described by (
1.6
) is that of a stable
C^{0,\frac{1}{3}}
pre-shock, which recovers the known results (see e.g.
[
9
,
6
,
39
]
). For
n\geq 2
,
\mathcal{M}_{n}
is the graph of a Lipschitz function from a codimension-
(2n-2)
subspace to
\mathbb{R}^{2n-2}
.

(ii)

In the case
n=2
,
\mathcal{M}_{2}
is a codimension-
2
submanifold of
(W^{6,\infty}(\mathbb{T}))^{3}
containing the point
(\overline{u}_{0},\overline{\sigma}_{0},\overline{S}_{0})
, and the expansion (
1.6
) describes the cusp structure of a
C^{0,\frac{1}{5}}
pre-shock. This recovers (with a different proof) the result in
[
8
]
, under milder regularity and smallness assumptions on the initial data (e.g.
W^{6,\infty}
instead of
C^{8}
).

(iii)

The intuition behind the cusp-expansion (
1.6
) stems from an exact computation performed on the 1D Burgers equation (presented in §
2
below) and the transport-structure of the evolution equation satisfied by the Riemann variables (see §
3.1
below). At this stage we just note that if the initial data of the one-dimensional Euler equation is precisely the smooth function
(\overline{u}_{0},\overline{\sigma}_{0},\overline{S}_{0}):=\frac{1}{2}(\overline{w}_{0},\overline{w}_{0},0)
, which corresponds to the limiting case
\varepsilon\to 0
at fixed
n\geq 1
, then for all
t\in(0,T_{*})
the solution
(u,\sigma,S)(\cdot,t)
of (
1.5
) is given by
\frac{1}{2}(\overline{w},\overline{w},0)(\cdot,t)
, where
\overline{w}
solves the 1D Burgers equation
\partial_{t}\overline{w}+\frac{1+\alpha}{2}\overline{w}\partial_{y}\overline{w}=0
, with initial data
\overline{w}_{0}
. Now since
\overline{w}_{0}^{\prime}(y)=-1+y^{2n}
for
y
close to
0
, and
\overline{w}_{0}^{\prime}(y)\geq-1+C
for
y
far from
0
(for some
C>0
), the 1D Burgers equation develops a gradient singularity at exactly time
T_{*}=\frac{2}{1+\alpha}
, at exactly the location
y_{*}=\frac{2}{1+\alpha}\overline{w}_{0}(0)
, and the Puiseux expansion
\overline{w}(y,T_{*})=\overline{w}_{0}(0)-2(2n+1)^{\frac{1}{2n+1}}(y-y_{*})^{\frac{1}{2n+1}}+\mathcal{O}_{n}(|y-y_{*}|^{\frac{1}{2n+1}})
holds for all
y
sufficiently close to
y_{*}
. For 1D Burgers these facts were established earlier in
[
23
, Proposition 9]
using self-similar techniques.

(iv)

If further regularity assumptions are placed on the initial conditions, such as
W^{2n+2+L,\infty}
for some
L\geq 1
, then the
\mathcal{O}_{\gamma,n}(|y-y_{*}|^{\frac{2}{2n+1}})
term present in (
1.6a
)–(
1.6b
) and the
\mathcal{O}_{\gamma,n}(\varepsilon|y-y_{*}|^{1+\frac{1}{2n+1}})
term present in (
1.6c
) may be further expanded as

(y-y_{*})^{\frac{2}{2n+1}}
, respectively
(y-y_{*})^{1+\frac{1}{2n+1}}
multiplied by an

(L-1)^{\rm th}
order Taylor polynomial in the variable
(y-y_{*})^{\frac{1}{2n+1}}
(a truncated Puiseux series). In fact, our proof (see §
9
and Appendix
A
) gives an algorithm for computing the coefficients of this truncated Puiseux series, resulting in explicit expressions of these coefficients similar to that for
\mathsf{b}
, up to an
\mathcal{O}_{\gamma,n}(\varepsilon)
correction. To sum up, if the initial data is assumed to have
L
more bounded derivatives, then (
1.6
) may be expanded to
L
orders higher, with controlled coefficients and error bounds. For conciseness, in this paper we choose not to pursue these higher order expansions.

(v)

We have chosen to pay great attention to how the parameter
n
enters the description about the Euler solution on the pre-shock; this is the main reason for the extended length of the paper. For instance, the estimate for the blowup time
T_{*}=\nicefrac{{2}}{{(1+\alpha)}}+\mathcal{O}_{\gamma}(\varepsilon)
is independent of
n
. A much more subtle and delicate issue is the description of the parameter
\mathsf{b}
appearing in (
1.6
) — to leading order in
\varepsilon
it is given by an explicit formula,
(2n+1)^{\frac{1}{2n+1}}
. Equally subtle is the description of the range of
y
(close to
y_{*}
) for which (
1.6
) holds — the implicit constant in
|y-y_{*}|^{\frac{1}{2n+1}}\lesssim 1
is independent of
n
(the precise bound is
\leq\nicefrac{{1}}{{C_{0}}}
, where
C_{0}
depends only on the choice of
\overline{w}_{0}
). These
n
-independent bounds are important for Remark
1.2
below.

Remark 1.2

(
The Riemann data and the infinitely unstable limit
n\to\infty
).

Consider the limit as
n\to\infty
of the function
\mathsf{E}_{n}(y):=\mathsf{a}-\mathsf{b}_{n}(y-y_{*})^{\frac{1}{2n+1}}
, where
\mathsf{b}_{n}:=(2n+1)^{\frac{1}{2n+1}}
and
\mathsf{a}>0
are constants. In view of item
(v)
in the above remarks, the function
\mathsf{E}_{n}
precisely matches the cusp expressions (
1.6a
) and (
1.6b
) appearing in Theorem
1.1
. While the pointwise limit may be easily computed as
\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathsf{E}_{n}(y):=\mathsf{a}-\mathrm{sgn\,}(y-y_{*})
, which we recognize as the standard Riemann data of 1D gas dynamics (with left state
\mathsf{a}+1
and right state
\mathsf{a}-1
), the constraint
|y-y_{*}|^{\frac{1}{2n+1}}\leq\nicefrac{{1}}{{C_{0}}}
present in (
1.6
) (see also item
(v)
above) means that the range of validity of this limit is trivial (
y=y_{*}
). We thus highlight the fact that while formally the Puiseux series expansion at the pre-shock converges to the Riemann data in the infinitely unstable limit
n\to\infty
, drawing a direct connection between the solutions of 1D Euler constructed in Theorem
1.1
(as
n\to\infty
) and the solution of the classical Riemann problem for 1D Euler, remains to date elusive.

1.4.
Outline and new ideas

As we have already noted, the proof of Theorem
1.1
relies on the analysis of differentiated Riemann variables in the coordinates of the fast-acoustic characteristics. When coupled to energy estimates and geometric considerations, this perspective has proven itself to be fundamental in analyzing shock formation for multi-dimensional Euler in
[
42
]
.
The proof of Theorem
1.1
proceeds as follows:

•

First, we change variables and study the 1D Euler system in
differentiated Riemann variables
(see §
3.1
) which allows us to include non-trivial entropy evolution without any derivative loss. Then, we change coordinates by analyzing the system when the functions are composed with the
fast acoustic characteristics

\eta
of the system (see §
4.1
). In these new
Lagrangian-type coordinates
, the dominant Riemann variable is effectively “frozen”, analogous to how the solution of Burgers equation is frozen along its corresponding characteristics (see §
2
). By making this change to Lagrangian-type coordinates, we are able to reduce the task of controlling the derivatives of the solution to that of controlling the derivatives of the subdominant Riemann variable, and the entropy (see e.g. §
5.2
and §
5.4
).

•

Second, we show in §
4
that for a broad class of initial data the solution forms a singularity in finite time, and that this singularity is characterized by the flow
\eta
of the fast acoustic characteristics becoming degenerate; that is
\eta_{x}\to 0
at a distinguished point in spacetime.

•

Third, in §
5
we prove that
in Lagrangian coordinates the solution stays as smooth as the initial data
, uniformly up to the time of the first singularity. To do this, we prove novel
L^{q}
energy estimates for the time derivatives of the system which are uniform in
1<q<\infty
, and then send
q\rightarrow\infty
to obtain
L^{\infty}
bounds. After we have obtained
L^{\infty}
bounds on the time derivatives, the fact that the wave speeds are uniformly transverse to one another (since we are bounded away from vacuum) allows us extract bounds on mixed space-time derivatives from the time derivatives.

•

Next, in §
6
we establish stability estimates for the Euler solution with respect to perturbations of the initial data. For instance, if the initial data for the dominant Riemann variable is given by
w_{0}=\overline{w}_{0}+\lambda\widetilde{w}_{0}
, for some parameter
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
and with
\overline{w}_{0}
,
\widetilde{w}_{0}
fixed, we compute
\partial_{\lambda}
of the resulting one-parameter family of solutions and establish uniform-in-
\lambda
bounds. In §
7
we bound the difference of two solutions in terms of the size of the difference of their initial data in
(W^{2n+2,\infty}(\mathbb{T}))^{3}
.

•

After this, in §
8
we use the implicit function theorem to show that for initial data in a given open subset of
(W^{2n+2,\infty}(\mathbb{T}))^{3}
there exists a solution
(x_{*},T_{*})
of the system

\eta_{x}(x_{*},T_{*})=0\,,\qquad\partial_{x}\eta_{x}(x_{*},T_{*})=0\,,\qquad\ldots\qquad\partial^{2n-1}_{x}\eta_{x}(x_{*},T_{*})=0\,,

if and only if this data lies on a codimension-
(2n-2)
Banach submanifold of
(W^{2n+2,\infty}(\mathbb{T}))^{3}
. Because this system has
2n
equations and only
2
unknowns, this is precisely what one would expect. For all such data where a solution
(x_{*},T_{*})
exists, the solution is then shown to be unique.

•

Finally, in §
9
we invert the fast-acoustic flow map
\eta
at the site of the pre-shock using a
Puiseux series
, to obtain a description of the solution in the original Eulerian coordinates. Because in coordinates adapted to the fast acoustic characteristic, the solution remains as smooth as the initial data up to the time of the first gradient blowup, the regularity of the solution
(w,z,k)
in Eulerian variables is determined by the regularity of the inverse flow map
\eta^{-1}
.

1.5.
Notation

Throughout the paper we shall appeal to the following notational conventions:

•

The notation
A\lesssim B
is used to say that there exists a constant
C>0
, such that
A\leq CB
. Here, the implicit constant
C
is assumed to be
independent
of the parameters
\alpha,n,\varepsilon
,
independent
of
(x,t)\in\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
,
independent
of the constant
C_{0}\geq 3
(appearing in (
3.6
)), and
independent
of our choice of
(w_{0},z_{0},k_{0})\in\mathcal{A}_{n}(\varepsilon,C_{0})
(defined in (
3.6
)).

•

The notation
A\lesssim_{F}B
is used to indicate that there exists a constant
C=C(F)>0
, such that
A\leq CB
; that is, the implicit constant depends on the parameter
F
.

•

We write
A=\mathcal{O}(B)
when
|A|\lesssim B
, and
A=\mathcal{O}_{F}(B)
when
|A|\lesssim_{F}B
.

•

We write “If
A\ll B
then
X
” to mean that there exists a constant
C\geq 1
, independent of
\alpha,n,\varepsilon,x,t,C_{0}
and independent of our choice of
(w_{0},z_{0},k_{0})\in\mathcal{A}_{n}(\varepsilon,C_{0})
, such that “if
C\cdot A\leq B
, then
X
is true”.

•

If
f
is a function defined on
\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
, and
\eta
is a map defined on
\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
, we will write “
f\circ\eta
” to denote the function

f\!\circ\!\eta(x,t):=f(\eta(x,t),t).

•

We use the notation
\mathbbm{1}_{A}
to denote the characteristic function of a set
A
.

•

We write
\partial_{y}f,\partial_{x}f,\partial_{t}f
to denote the partial derivative of a function
f
with respect to
y,x,
and
t
respectively. If
f
is a function of
(x,t)
, and
\beta=(\beta_{x},\beta_{t})
is a multi-index, we write
\partial^{\beta}f
to denote
\partial^{\beta_{x}}_{x}\partial^{\beta_{t}}_{t}f
. There are a few exceptions where we will use subscript notation to denote derivatives:
\eta_{x}:=\partial_{x}\eta,\eta_{xt}:=\partial_{x}\partial_{t}\eta,\Sigma_{t}:=\partial_{t}\Sigma,\Sigma_{x}:=\partial_{x}\Sigma,(\eta_{x}\mathring{W})_{t}:=\partial_{t}(\eta_{x}\mathring{W}),(\Sigma^{-1})_{t}:=\partial_{t}(\Sigma^{-1}),(\Sigma^{-1})_{x}:=\partial_{x}(\Sigma^{-1})
. In §
6
we will also use subscript notation to denote a derivative with respect to a parameter
\lambda
introduced in §
6
, except when
x
or
t
is already being used as a subscript; so, for example, we will write
\partial^{\beta}\mathring{K}_{\lambda}
to mean
\partial^{\beta_{x}}_{x}\partial^{\beta_{t}}_{t}\partial_{\lambda}\mathring{K}
but we will write
\partial_{\lambda}\eta_{x}
to denote
\partial_{x}\partial_{\lambda}\eta
.

•

We write
w^{\prime}_{0},z^{\prime}_{0},
and
k^{\prime}_{0}
to denote the first derivatives of
w_{0},z_{0},
and
k_{0}
respectively.

2.
Intuition from the Burgers equation

This section aims to revisit some well-known aspects of the 1D Burgers equation from a specific perspective that will inform our proof for the 1D Euler equation. Though the examples presented are not novel, they offer valuable intuition; moreover, the 1D Burgers equation is embedded in the 1D Euler system. Indeed, upon inspecting (
1.5
), we see that if
S|_{t=0}\equiv 0
, then
S(\cdot,t)\equiv 0
for all times
t
prior to the first gradient singularity. In this case, the remaining equations (
1.5a
)–(
1.5b
) have a particular solution: if
u|_{t=0}=\sigma|_{t=0}
and
S|_{t=0}\equiv 0
, then the relation
u(\cdot,t)\equiv\sigma(\cdot,t)
holds for all times
t
prior to the first gradient singularity. Denoting
u+\sigma=2u=2\sigma
by
w
, we see that in this special case, (
3.2
) reduces to:

(2.1)

\partial_{t}w+\tfrac{1+\alpha}{2}w\partial_{y}w=0,

Rescaling time with
t\rightarrow\tfrac{1+\alpha}{2}t
, this becomes the classical
inviscid Burgers equation

(2.2)

\partial_{t}w+w\partial_{y}w=0,

the archetypal equation for shock formation and development.

Classically, (
2.2
) is solved by the method of characteristics. If
w
is a solution of (
2.2
) on
\mathbb{R}\times[0,T]
and we define the
characteristics

\eta
to be the flow of of the scalar field
w
, namely as the solution of

\tfrac{d}{dt}\eta(x,t)=w(\eta(x,t),t),\qquad\eta(x,0)=x,

then
w
must remain constant along the flow lines of
\eta
. From this it follows that if
w_{0}(x):=w(x,0)
then

(2.3)

\eta(x,t)=x+tw_{0}(x).

Thus, characteristics are straight lines in spacetime, and the line originating at the spacetime point
(x,0)
has slope
w_{0}(x)
. Conversely, if we are given initial data
w_{0}
and we define
\eta
via (
2.3
), then one can construct a unique solution
w
via the implicit equation

w(\eta(x,t),t)=w_{0}(x)

at all points
(y,t)
such that the equation

(2.4)

(y,t)=(\eta(x,t),t)

has a unique solution
x\in\mathbb{R}
.
For initial data
w_{0}
that is not monotone increasing in
x
, it is clear that (
2.4
) will not be uniquely solvable for all
(y,t)\in\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty)
, resulting in a gradient singularity at some finite time
T_{*}\in(0,\infty)
. In particular, when
w_{0}\in C^{1}_{\text{loc}}(\mathbb{R})
, the maximal time of existence and uniqueness of the
C^{1}
solution
w
of (
2.2
) is

T_{*}:=\begin{cases}-\frac{1}{\inf w^{\prime}_{0}}&\inf w^{\prime}_{0}<0\,,\\
+\infty&\inf w^{\prime}_{0}\geq 0\,.\end{cases}

Note that for all prior times
0\leq t<T_{*}
we have

\eta_{x}(x,t)=1+tw^{\prime}_{0}(x)>0,

and hence
\eta(\cdot,t):\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a
C^{1}
diffeomorphism, but at the point
(x_{*},T_{*})
where
\eta_{x}(x_{*},t)\rightarrow 0
as
t\rightarrow T_{*}
, we have
\partial_{y}w(\eta(x_{*},t),t)=w_{0}^{\prime}(x_{*})(\eta_{x}(x_{*},t))^{-1}\rightarrow-\infty
. Therefore, the first singularity in a
C^{1}
solution of Burgers equation is characterized by
\eta
ceasing to be a
C^{1}
diffeomorphism, i.e. the function
\eta_{x}
attaining a zero.

Now suppose that
w_{0}
is smooth,
w^{\prime}_{0}
attains its global minimum at a point
x_{*}
, and
w^{\prime}_{0}(x_{*})<0
. Our objective is to give a precise description
of the solution
w
near the pre-shock
(y_{*},T_{*})=(\eta(x_{*},T_{*}),T_{*})
. Generically,
x_{*}
is a
non-degenerate
local minimum of
w_{0}^{\prime}
, i.e. the second derivative test of calculus applies:

\partial_{x}w^{\prime}_{0}(x_{*})=0,\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\partial^{2}_{x}w^{\prime}_{0}(x_{*})>0\,.

In this case, from (
2.3
) we deduce

\eta_{x}(x_{*},T_{*})=0,\qquad\partial_{x}\eta_{x}(x_{*},T_{*})=T_{*}\partial_{x}w^{\prime}_{0}(x_{*})=0,\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\partial^{2}_{x}\eta_{x}(x_{*},T_{*})=T_{*}\partial^{2}_{x}w^{\prime}_{0}(x_{*})=:3!a_{3}>0.

Therefore, assuming that
w_{0}\in C^{4}
, Taylor’s theorem yields

\eta(x,T_{*})=\eta(x_{*},T_{*})+a_{3}(x-x_{*})^{3}[1+\mathcal{O}(|x-x_{*}|)]\,.

If
y=\eta(x,T_{*})
and
y_{*}=\eta(x_{*},T_{*})
, then for
y
near
y_{*}
we have

x-x_{*}=\big(\tfrac{y-y_{*}}{a_{3}}\big)^{1/3}+\mathcal{O}(|y-y_{*}|^{2/3}),

and thus

\displaystyle w(y,T_{*})=w(\eta(x,T_{*}),T_{*})=w_{0}(x)

\displaystyle=w_{0}(x_{*})+\tfrac{w^{\prime}_{0}(x_{*})}{1!}(x-x_{*})+\mathcal{O}((x-x_{*})^{2})

(2.5)

\displaystyle=w(y_{*},T_{*})+w^{\prime}_{0}(x_{*})\big(\tfrac{y-y_{*}}{a_{3}}\big)^{1/3}+\mathcal{O}(|y-y_{*}|^{2/3}).

Thus, the Taylor series expansion of the smooth function
(w\circ\eta)(\cdot,T_{*})=w_{0}(\cdot)
with respect to
x-x_{*}
, becomes a
fractional series expansion
of the Hölder continuous function
w(\cdot,T_{*})
with respect to
(y-y_{*})^{1/3}
. Since by assumption we have that
w_{0}^{\prime}(x_{*})<0
, the expansion (
2.5
) shows that

w(\cdot,T_{*})
forms a
C^{0,\frac{1}{3}}
-cusp at
(y_{*},T_{*})
, resembling

-y^{1/3}.

The above described behavior is moreover
stable
under small
W^{4,\infty}
perturbations to
w_{0}
, because all functions
w_{0}
close enough to our original function in
W^{4,\infty}
are going to be such that
w^{\prime}_{0}
attains its minimum at a point nearby
x_{*}
, the minimum will still be negative, and it will still be a non-degenerate critical point of
w^{\prime}_{0}
.

Consider next the case when
w_{0}^{\prime}
attains its global minimum at a
degenerate
critical point
x_{*}
, and the derivatives of
w_{0}^{\prime}
do not vanish to infinite order at
x_{*}
. Then, necessarily there would exist some integer
n\geq 2
such that

\partial^{i}_{x}w^{\prime}_{0}(x_{*})=0\;\mbox{ for all }\;i\in\{1,\ldots,2n-1\},\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\partial^{2n}_{x}w^{\prime}_{0}(x_{*})>0\,.

The integer
n
is sometimes referred to as the
order of flatness
of
w_{0}^{\prime}
at the critical point
x_{*}
.
In this case, the same arguments as before would imply that

x-x_{*}=\big(\tfrac{y-y_{*}}{a_{2n+1}}\big)^{\frac{1}{2n+1}}+\mathcal{O}(|y-y_{*}|^{\frac{2}{2n+1}})\,,

where

1+T_{*}w_{0}^{\prime}(x_{*})=0\,,\quad T_{*}\partial_{x}w_{0}^{\prime}(x_{*})=0\,,\quad\ldots\quad T_{*}\partial_{x}^{2n-1}w_{0}^{\prime}(x_{*})=0\,,\quad T_{*}\partial_{x}^{2n}w_{0}^{\prime}(x_{*})=:(2n+1)!a_{2n+1}>0\,.

The result is that
w(\cdot,T_{*})
forms a
C^{0,\frac{1}{2n+1}}
-cusp at
(y_{*},T_{*})
, resembling

-y^{\frac{1}{2n+1}}.

Such a behavior, however, is
unstable
under smooth perturbations.

For example, the prototypical function which attains a global minimum at a degenerate critical point
x_{*}
, and whose derivative exhibits flatness of order
2n
at
x_{*}
, is given by

(2.6)

\overline{w}_{0}(x)=-x+\tfrac{1}{2n+1}x^{2n+1}\,,

for which with
x_{*}=0
. When
n\geq 2
, the
C^{\infty}
-smooth and locally small (take
0<\varepsilon\leq 1
small) perturbation
w_{0}(x):=\overline{w}_{0}(x)+\frac{1}{3}\varepsilon x^{3}
represents a function which attains its global minimum at
x_{*}(\varepsilon):=0
, but now this global minimum is non-degenerate since
\partial_{x}^{2}w_{0}^{\prime}(x_{*}(\varepsilon))=2\varepsilon>0
. A more interesting example is given by the
C^{\infty}
-smooth and locally small perturbation
w_{0}(x):=\overline{w}_{0}(x)-\frac{1}{3}\varepsilon x^{3}
, which attains its global minimum simultaneously at two points
x_{*}(\varepsilon):=\pm(\nicefrac{{\varepsilon}}{{n}})^{\frac{1}{2n-2}}
, and each of these global minima are non-degenerate since
\partial_{x}^{2}w_{0}^{\prime}(x_{*}(\varepsilon))=2\varepsilon(2n-2)>0
.
These examples provide the simple reason behind the instability of
C^{0,\frac{1}{2n+1}}
-cusp singularities for 1D Burgers.

Next, we discuss the finite codimension stability of
C^{0,\frac{1}{2n+1}}
-cusp singularities for 1D Burgers dynamics, from a perspective which will turn out to be useful when discussing 1D Euler. Recall the function
\overline{w}_{0}
defined in (
2.6
),
and let
\overline{w}
be the solution of 1D Burgers (
2.2
) on
\mathbb{R}
with initial data
\overline{w}_{0}
. Since
\overline{w}^{\prime}_{0}
has a unique global minimum at
x_{*}=0
, and at this point
\overline{w}_{0}^{\prime}(0)=-1
, the solution
\overline{w}
develops a singularity at time
T_{*}=1
. At the blowup time, we have

\eta(x,1)=\tfrac{1}{2n+1}x^{2n+1}\,,

and therefore if
y=\eta(x,1)
, so that
y_{*}=\eta(x_{*},1)=0
, then

x=((2n+1)y)^{\frac{1}{2n+1}}\,,

resulting in the precise and global description of the
C^{0,\frac{1}{2n+1}}
-cusp at
y=0
:

(2.7)

\overline{w}(y,1)=\overline{w}_{0}(x)=-(2n+1)^{\frac{1}{2n+1}}y^{\frac{1}{2n+1}}+y.

The above scenario is in fact stable under suitable perturbations.

Proposition 2.1

(Stability and finite codimension-stability)
.

Let
n\geq 1
be an integer, let
\overline{w}_{0}
be defined as in (
2.6
), and define for
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
the ball

U^{n}_{\varepsilon}:=\{w_{0}:\|w_{0}-\overline{w}_{0}\|_{C^{2n+1}}<\varepsilon\}\,.

Then there is a codimension-
(2n-2)
Banach manifold
\mathcal{M}_{n}
of initial data in
U^{n}_{\varepsilon}
such that for all data
w_{0}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
the unique solution
w
has a first singularity at a unique point
(y_{*},T_{*})=(0,1)+\mathcal{O}_{n}(\varepsilon)
and at that point
w
of (
2.2
) forms a cusp resembling (
2.7
).

Proof.

Since
\partial^{2n}_{x}w^{\prime}_{0}(x)\geq(2n)!-\varepsilon
uniformly in
x
for all
w_{0}\in U^{n}_{\varepsilon}
, there is a unique
\mathring{x}
in
\mathbb{R}
such that

\partial^{2n-1}_{x}w^{\prime}_{0}(\mathring{x})=0
, and
\mathring{x}
satisfies
|\mathring{x}|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{(2n)!-\varepsilon}
. In the case
n=1
,
\mathring{x}
is the unique critical point of
w^{\prime}_{0}
and is its global minimum. In the case
n\geq 2
, define the function
f:U^{n}_{\varepsilon}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2n-2}
,

f(w_{0}):=\begin{bmatrix}\partial_{x}w^{\prime}_{0}(\mathring{x})\\
\vdots\\
\partial^{2n-2}_{x}w^{\prime}_{0}(\mathring{x})\end{bmatrix}

If
f(w_{0})=(0,0\ldots,0)
, then Taylor expanding
w^{\prime}_{0}
about
x=\mathring{x}
gives us

w^{\prime}_{0}(x)\geq w^{\prime}_{0}(\mathring{x})+[(2n)!-\varepsilon](x-\mathring{x})^{2n}

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
, so
\mathring{x}
is the unique global minimum of
w^{\prime}_{0}
. If we define
\mathcal{M}_{1}=U^{1}_{\varepsilon}
, and
\mathcal{M}_{n}=\{f=0\}
for
n\geq 2
, then it is straightforward to show that at time
-1/w_{0}^{\prime}(\mathring{x})
the solution
w
of (
2.2
) with initial data
w_{0}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
, develops a cusp resembling
-y^{\frac{1}{2n+1}}
.

When
n\geq 2
, we can observe that
\mathcal{M}_{n}
is a Banach manifold using the Implicit Function Theorem. All
w_{0}\in U^{n}_{\varepsilon}
can be decomposed as

w_{0}(x)=\overline{w}_{0}(x)+\big(\tfrac{\lambda_{1}}{2!}x^{2}+\tfrac{\lambda_{2}}{3!}x^{3}+\ldots+\tfrac{\lambda_{2n-2}}{(2n-1)!}x^{2n-1}\big)\chi(x)+\widetilde{w}_{0}(x)

where
\chi
is a
C^{\infty}
bump function with

\mathbbm{1}_{[-1,1]}\leq\chi\leq\mathbbm{1}_{[-3,3]}
,
the
\{\lambda_{j}\}_{j=1}^{2n-2}
are sufficiently small (with respect to
\varepsilon
and
n
) real numbers,
and
\widetilde{w}_{0}
is a sufficiently small (with respect to
\varepsilon
and
n
)
C^{2n+1}
function satisfying
\partial^{j}_{x}\widetilde{w}_{0}^{\prime}(0)=0
for
j=1,\ldots,2n-2
.
To see this, simply define
\lambda_{j}:=\partial_{x}^{j}w_{0}^{\prime}(0)
, and then declare
\widetilde{w}_{0}
to be the remainder in the above formula; since
\partial_{x}^{j}\overline{w}_{0}^{\prime}(0)=0
for all
j=1,\ldots,2n-2
, it is clear that
\widetilde{w}_{0}
will then satisfy
\partial^{j}_{x}\widetilde{w}_{0}^{\prime}(0)=0
for
j=1,\ldots,2n-2
. Viewing the critical point
\mathring{x}
as a function of
\{\lambda_{j}\}_{j=1}^{2n-2}
and
\widetilde{w}_{0}
, and differentiating the expression
\partial^{2n-1}_{x}w^{\prime}_{0}(\mathring{x})=0
respect to
\lambda_{j}
, taking into account that both
w_{0}^{\prime}
and
\mathring{x}
are implicitly functions of
\lambda_{j}
, and using that
\chi^{\prime}(\mathring{x})=0
, we deduce

\partial^{2n}_{x}w^{\prime}_{0}(\mathring{x})\tfrac{\partial\mathring{x}}{\partial\lambda_{j}}=0\,,\qquad\mbox{for all}\qquad j=1,\ldots,2n-2\,.

From the positive lower bound
\partial^{2n}_{x}w^{\prime}_{0}(\mathring{x})\geq(2n)!-\varepsilon
it follows that
\frac{\partial\mathring{x}}{\partial\lambda_{j}}=0
for all
j=1,\ldots,2n-2
. Therefore, writing the
i^{\rm th}
component of the function
f
as
f^{i}=\partial_{x}^{i}w_{0}^{\prime}(\mathring{x})
, and using that
\frac{\partial\mathring{x}}{\partial\lambda_{j}}=0
, we may similarly obtain

\tfrac{\partial f_{i}}{\partial\lambda_{j}}=\partial^{i+1}_{x}\overline{w}^{\prime}_{0}(\mathring{x})\tfrac{\partial\mathring{x}}{\partial\lambda_{j}}+\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\lambda_{j}}\Bigl(\displaystyle{\sum}_{k=i}^{2n-2}\tfrac{\lambda_{k}}{(k-i)!}\mathring{x}^{k-i}\Bigr)+\partial^{i+1}_{x}\widetilde{w}^{\prime}_{0}(\mathring{x})\tfrac{\partial\mathring{x}}{\partial\lambda_{j}}=\tfrac{\mathring{x}^{j-i}}{(j-i)!}\mathbbm{1}_{j\geq i}\,,

for all
i,j=1,\ldots,2n-2
. Since
|\mathring{x}|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{(2n)!-\varepsilon}
, it follows that

D_{\lambda}f=\mathrm{Id\,}+\mathcal{O}_{n}(\varepsilon)

everywhere in
U^{n}_{\varepsilon}
. Therefore, for each choice of
\widetilde{w}_{0}
(lying in a sufficiently small ball, of radius related to
\varepsilon
and
n
, in
C^{2n+1}
) there is a unique choice of
(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{2n-2})
such that
f(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{2n-2},\widetilde{w}_{0})=0
, i.e., such that the corresponding
w_{0}
lies in
\mathcal{M}_{n}
. The Implicit Function Theorem yields that his choice of
\{\lambda_{j}\}_{j=1}^{2n-2}
is given by a
C^{1}
function of
\widetilde{w}_{0}
(in the sense of Fréchet derivatives).
∎

3.
The differentiated Riemann variables, the initial data, and a blowup criterion

Returning to the 1D Euler equations (
1.5
), in this section we introduce the
classical and the differentiated Riemann-type variables which are used in our analysis, we precisely characterize the set of initial conditions with which we work with, and we give a characterization of the time
T_{*}
of the first gradient singularity.

3.1.
Classical and differentiated Riemann variables

For many first-order hyperbolic systems in one space dimension, it can be fruitful to analyze the PDE in terms of
Riemann variables
, which in a sense diagonalize the system. For the 1D Euler equations, the Riemann variables
w
and
z
are defined as follows:

(3.1)

\displaystyle w:=u+\sigma\,,\qquad z:=u-\sigma\,,\qquad k:=S.

Above we have also introduced the notation
k
for the specific entropy
S
in order to distinguish the Riemann set of variables
(w,z,k)
from the classical ones,
(u,\sigma,S)
.
With respect to the
(w,z,k)
variables the 1D Euler equations (
1.5
) become

(3.2a)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}w+\lambda_{3}\partial_{y}w

\displaystyle=\tfrac{\alpha}{2\gamma}\sigma^{2}\partial_{y}k,

(3.2b)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}z+\lambda_{1}\partial_{y}z

\displaystyle=\tfrac{\alpha}{2\gamma}\sigma^{2}\partial_{y}k,

(3.2c)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}k+\lambda_{2}\partial_{y}k

\displaystyle=0,

where

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}

\displaystyle:=u-\alpha\sigma=\tfrac{1-\alpha}{2}w+\tfrac{1+\alpha}{2}z,

\displaystyle\lambda_{2}

\displaystyle:=u=\tfrac{1}{2}w+\tfrac{1}{2}z,

\displaystyle\lambda_{3}

\displaystyle:=u+\alpha\sigma=\tfrac{1+\alpha}{2}w+\tfrac{1-\alpha}{2}z.

The functions
\lambda_{i}
are the
wave speeds
of the system. Most important to us will be
\lambda_{3}=u+\alpha\sigma=u+c
, which is the
fast acoustic wave speed
. The
fast acoustic characteristics
are denoted by
\eta
and are as the solutions of the ODE via

(3.3)

\partial_{t}\eta(x,t)=\lambda_{3}(\eta(x,t),t),\qquad\eta(x,0)=0\,.

That is,
\eta
is the flow of the fast acoustic wave speed.

Because the 1D Euler equations have both Galilean transformations and additions of a constant entropy as symmetries, all of the essential dynamics of (
1.5
) are captured by studying
\sigma,\partial_{y}u,\partial_{y}\sigma
, and
\partial_{y}S
. Therefore, it makes sense to study (
3.2
) in terms of
\sigma,\partial_{y}w,\partial_{y}z,
and
\partial_{y}k
. If one takes
\partial_{y}
of the system (
3.2
), and diagonalizes the system (omitting terms which are quadratic or cubic in
\partial_{y}w,\partial_{y}z,
and
\partial_{y}k
), one arrives at the
differentiated Riemann variables

(\mathring{w},\mathring{z},\mathring{k})
, defined via

(3.4a)

\displaystyle\mathring{w}

\displaystyle:=\partial_{y}w-\tfrac{1}{2\gamma}\sigma\partial_{y}k,

(3.4b)

\displaystyle\mathring{z}

\displaystyle:=\partial_{y}z+\tfrac{1}{2\gamma}\sigma\partial_{y}k,

(3.4c)

\displaystyle\mathring{k}

\displaystyle:=\partial_{y}k,

and which satisfy the evolution equations

(3.5a)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathring{w}+\lambda_{3}\partial_{y}\mathring{w}

\displaystyle=-\mathring{w}\partial_{y}\lambda_{3}+\tfrac{\alpha}{4\gamma}\sigma\mathring{k}(\mathring{w}+\mathring{z})=-\mathring{w}(\tfrac{1+\alpha}{2}\mathring{w}+\tfrac{1-\alpha}{2}\mathring{z}+\tfrac{\alpha}{2\gamma}\sigma\mathring{k})+\tfrac{\alpha}{4\gamma}\sigma\mathring{k}(\mathring{w}+\mathring{z})

(3.5b)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathring{z}+\lambda_{1}\partial_{y}\mathring{z}

\displaystyle=-\mathring{z}\partial_{y}\lambda_{1}-\tfrac{\alpha}{4\gamma}\sigma\mathring{k}(\mathring{w}+\mathring{z})=-\mathring{z}(\tfrac{1-\alpha}{2}\mathring{w}+\tfrac{1+\alpha}{2}\mathring{z}-\tfrac{\alpha}{2\gamma}\sigma\mathring{k})-\tfrac{\alpha}{4\gamma}\sigma\mathring{k}(\mathring{w}+\mathring{z})

(3.5c)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathring{k}+\lambda_{2}\partial_{y}\mathring{k}

\displaystyle=-\mathring{k}\partial_{y}\lambda_{2}=-\mathring{k}(\tfrac{1}{2}\mathring{w}+\tfrac{1}{2}\mathring{z})\,.

The differentiated Riemann variables will allow us to study the evolution of the system without derivative loss; indeed, it is evident from (
3.5
) that these are three coupled transport equations with right hand sides which are polynomial expressions of the unknowns (see also (
4.5
) below). The “ring super-index” in the notation of (
3.4
) is present to signify that differentiation has taken place; this notation is chosen for consistency with
[
42
]
.

For the remainder of this paper, we will study the Cauchy problem for the system (
3.2
) and (
3.5
). Next, we describe the initial data for these systems, by specifying the set
\mathcal{A}_{n}(\varepsilon,C_{0})
where
(w_{0},z_{0},k_{0})
lies. The functions
(\mathring{w}_{0},\mathring{z}_{0},\mathring{k}_{0}):=(w_{0}^{\prime}-\frac{1}{2\gamma}\sigma_{0}k_{0}^{\prime},z_{0}^{\prime}+\frac{1}{2\gamma}\sigma_{0}k_{0}^{\prime},k_{0}^{\prime})
and
\sigma_{0}=\frac{1}{2}(w_{0}+z_{0})
are then defined as a consequence.

3.2.
Assumptions on the initial data

The domain of our functions will be
\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
, where
\mathbb{T}:=\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
is the torus. We will use the notation

|x-x^{\prime}|

to denote the distance between two points
x,x^{\prime}
on the torus. We will often implicitly identify the torus with the interval
[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})
when describing functions in a neighborhood of the point
0=0+\mathbb{Z}\in\mathbb{T}
.

Fix a constant
C_{0}\geq 3
and an integer
n\geq 1
. Assume that the initial data
(w_{0},z_{0},k_{0})
of (
3.2
) satisfies

(3.6a)

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}<\sigma_{0}=\tfrac{1}{2}(w_{0}+z_{0})

\displaystyle<2,

(3.6b)

\displaystyle-1-\varepsilon<\min_{\mathbb{T}}w^{\prime}_{0}

\displaystyle<-1+\varepsilon,

(3.6c)

\displaystyle\frac{\|\partial^{i+1}_{x}w_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}}{i!}

\displaystyle<C_{0}^{i}(1+\varepsilon)

\displaystyle i=0,\ldots,2n+1,

(3.6d)

\displaystyle\frac{\|\partial^{i+1}_{x}z_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}}{i!}

\displaystyle<C_{0}^{i}\varepsilon

\displaystyle i=0,\ldots,2n+1,

(3.6e)

\displaystyle\frac{\|\partial^{i+1}_{x}k_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}}{i!}

\displaystyle<C_{0}^{i}\varepsilon

\displaystyle i=0,\ldots,2n+1.

The constraints listed in (
3.6a
)–(
3.6e
)
define
an open set

(3.7)

\mathcal{A}_{n}(\varepsilon,C_{0})\subset(W^{2n+2,\infty}(\mathbb{T}))^{3}\,.

We will show that the solution
(w,z,k)
emanating from data in this open set all form a gradient singularity at roughly the same time, and we will prove uniform derivative estimates for all solutions with data in
\mathcal{A}_{n}(\varepsilon,C_{0})
.

The definition of
\mathcal{A}_{n}(\varepsilon,C_{0})
does not require any zeroth order constraints other than (
3.6a
) because the Euler equations have Galilean transformations and additions of constant entropy as symmetries (though they are not invariant under constant perturbations to the mass density). The constraint (
3.6b
) is not strictly necessary for the formation of the shock to occur, but is rather used to pin down the blowup time
T_{*}
(see §
3.3
for a definition of
T_{*}
); this follows from the time-rescaling symmetry of the Euler equations. In §
8.4
we will impose additional constraints on the initial data in order to say more about how the spatial location of the pre-shock is approximately determined by the initial data; since the Euler equations are invariant under spatial translations, we will not need such specifications until then.

3.3.
Local well-posedness and the Eulerian blowup criterion

The Euler equations (
1.5
) are locally well-posed in
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
for
s>\frac{3}{2}
. In particular, for
u_{0},\sigma_{0},S_{0}\in H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
with
\sigma_{0}>0
everywhere, there exists a positive
maximal time of existence

T_{*}\in(0,+\infty]
such that (
1.5
) has a unique
C^{1}_{y,t}
-smooth solution
(u,\sigma,S)
on
\mathbb{T}\times[0,T_{*})
, and furthermore

(u,\sigma,S)\in C([0,T_{*});(H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))^{3})\cap C^{1}([0,T_{*});(H^{s-1}(\mathbb{T}))^{3}).

Additionally,
T_{*}
can only be finite if: (a) the solution
(u,\sigma,S)
develops vacuum or implodes in finite time, meaning that either
\min_{y\in\mathbb{T}}\sigma(\cdot,t)\to 0
as
t\to T_{*}
or
\|(u,\sigma,S)(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}_{y}}\to\infty
as
t\to T_{*}
; (b) the solutions’ gradients blow up in finite time, at a non-integrable rate, meaning that it satisfies the
Eulerian blowup criterion

(3.8)

\int^{T_{*}}_{0}\Bigl(\|\partial_{y}u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\partial_{y}\sigma(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\partial_{y}S(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\Bigr)dt=+\infty\,.

These facts follow from the classical local well-posedness theory of symmetrizable quasilinear hyperbolic systems (see e.g.
[
25
, Chapter V]
, or
[
35
, Chapter 2]
).

For the 1D Euler equations (
1.5
), it is known that neither vacuum formation nor finite-time implosion is not possible on
\mathbb{T}\times[0,T_{*})
(see
[
14
,
13
]
and Proposition
4.1
for the initial data studied in this paper), for any choice of
u_{0},\sigma_{0},S_{0}\in H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
with
\sigma_{0}>0
everywhere and
s>\frac{3}{2}
; using this uniform upper and lower bound on the sound speed, one can show that
(u,\sigma,S)
remain uniformly bounded on
\mathbb{T}\times[0,T_{*})
. That is, option (a) above is not available to the 1D Euler
9
9

9

In multiple space dimensions, this is not the case: smooth finite-time implosions may be constructed
[
36
,
37
,
5
,
12
,
17
,
16
]
. The formation of vacuum in finite time, for smooth multi-dimensional Euler dynamics remains however to date open.
dynamics with smooth and non-vacuous initial conditions. Thus, the Eulerian blowup criterion (
3.8
) determines whether a finite-time singularity occurs at a time
T_{*}<\infty
.

The Eulerian blowup criterion (
3.8
) simultaneously serves as a continuation criterion for smooth solutions, and as a criterion for the propagation of higher regularity. Of relevance to this manuscript is the fact that if the initial data
u_{0},\sigma_{0},S_{0}\in W^{2n+2,\infty}(\mathbb{T})\subset H^{2}(\mathbb{T})
,
\sigma_{0}
is positive, and
T_{*}
is defined via (
3.8
) as the maximal time of existence of a
C^{1}_{y,t}
-smooth solution, then for all
t\in(0,T_{*})
we have that
\|(u,\sigma,S)(\cdot,t)\|_{W^{2n+2,\infty}(\mathbb{T})}<\infty
(naturally, the upper bound blows up as
t\to T_{*}
).

The Eulerian blowup criterion may also be stated in terms of Riemann variables. If
n\geq 1
, then for all initial data
w_{0},z_{0},k_{0}\in W^{2n+2,\infty}(\mathbb{T})
with
w_{0}-z_{0}>0
there exists a positive maximal time of existence
T_{*}\in(0,+\infty]
for which (
3.2
) has a unique
C^{1}_{y,t}
solution
(w,z,k)
on
\mathbb{T}\times[0,T_{*})
and if
T_{*}<\infty
then

(3.9)

\int^{T_{*}}_{0}\Bigl(\|\partial_{y}w(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\partial_{y}z(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\partial_{y}k(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\Bigr)dt=+\infty\,.

For the initial data
(w_{0},z_{0},k_{0})\in\mathcal{A}_{n}(\varepsilon,C_{0})
, we will prove that
T_{*}
is finite, so we will refer to
T_{*}
in this context as the
blowup time
.

4.
Initial estimates along the fast acoustic characteristic

Let
\eta
be the fast acoustic characteristics defined in (
3.3
). We will show that for all initial data
(w_{0},z_{0},k_{0})\in\mathcal{A}_{n}(\varepsilon,C_{0})
, the corresponding solution
(w,z,k)
of (
3.2
) stays as smooth as the initial data when precomposed with
\eta
, i.e. the functions
w\circ\eta,z\circ\eta,
and
k\circ\eta
stay as smooth as
w_{0},z_{0},
and
k_{0}

all the way up until its maximal time of existence,
T_{*}
. For all data in
\mathcal{A}_{n}(\varepsilon,C_{0})
, the maximal time of existence will be shown to be finite, and will be approximately determined as

T_{*}=\tfrac{2}{1+\alpha}+\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}(\varepsilon)\,.

Furthermore, the time
T_{*}
will be characterized as the first time when
\eta

ceases being a diffeomorphism
. That is,
T_{*}
will be equivalently characterized in terms of the
Lagrangian blowup criterion

\lim_{t\to T_{*}}\Bigl(\min_{x\in\mathbb{T}}\eta_{x}(x,t)\Bigr)=0\,.

If
(y,t)\in\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
are the
Eulerian variables
which the functions
w,z,k
take as inputs, we will define the
Lagrangian variables

(x,t)\in\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
via the equation

(4.1)

\displaystyle(y,t)=(\eta(x,t),t).

We will spend the rest of the paper studying the Euler system in terms of functions of these Lagrangian variables defined in §
4.1
. In §
4.2
, we prove that for all data in
\mathcal{A}_{n}(\varepsilon,C_{0})
the solution exists up until a time
T_{*}=\frac{2}{1+\alpha}+\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}(\varepsilon)
at which point
\eta_{x}
must have a zero, which corresponds to
\partial_{y}w
diverging to
-\infty
. Then in §
5
we will perform energy estimates in
L^{p}
for the higher order derivatives with respect to
x
and
t
. In §
6
we utilize similar energy estimates to quantify the stability of solutions with data in
\mathcal{A}_{n}(\varepsilon,C_{0})
with respect to small perturbations in
w_{0}
.

4.1.
The system in Lagrangian variables

With the differentiated Riemann variables
\mathring{w},\mathring{z},\mathring{k}
introduced in (
3.4
), we define

\Sigma:=\sigma\!\circ\!\eta\,,\qquad\mathring{W}:=\mathring{w}\!\circ\!\eta\,,\qquad\mathring{Z}:=\mathring{z}\!\circ\!\eta\,,\qquad\mathring{K}:=\mathring{k}\!\circ\!\eta\,.

These are functions of the Lagrangian variables
(x,t)
.

It is straightforward to compute that

(4.2a)

\displaystyle\Sigma_{t}

\displaystyle=-\alpha\Sigma\mathring{Z}+\tfrac{\alpha}{2\gamma}\Sigma^{2}\mathring{K},

(4.2b)

\displaystyle\Sigma_{x}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\eta_{x}\mathring{W}-\tfrac{1}{2}\eta_{x}\mathring{Z}+\tfrac{1}{2\gamma}\eta_{x}\Sigma\mathring{K},

(4.2c)

\displaystyle(\Sigma^{-1})_{t}

\displaystyle=\alpha\Sigma^{-1}\mathring{Z}-\tfrac{\alpha}{2\gamma}\mathring{K},

(4.2d)

\displaystyle\eta_{xt}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1+\alpha}{2}\eta_{x}\mathring{W}+\tfrac{1-\alpha}{2}\eta_{x}\mathring{Z}+\tfrac{\alpha}{2\gamma}\eta_{x}\Sigma\mathring{K}

(4.2e)

\displaystyle(\eta_{x}\mathring{W})_{t}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{\alpha}{4\gamma}\Sigma\mathring{K}(\eta_{x}\mathring{W}+\eta_{x}\mathring{Z}).

One can deduce from (
3.2
) that

\partial_{t}(k\!\circ\!\eta)=\alpha\Sigma\mathring{K}.

Letting
\partial_{x}
act on both sides of this equation, we have that

\partial_{t}(\eta_{x}\mathring{K})=\alpha\Sigma\partial_{x}\mathring{K}+\alpha\Sigma_{x}\mathring{K},\quad\Rightarrow\quad\eta_{x}\partial_{t}\mathring{K}=\alpha\Sigma\partial_{x}\mathring{K}+(\alpha\Sigma_{x}-\eta_{xt})\mathring{K}.

Plugging in (
4.2b
) and (
4.2d
) gives us

(4.3)

\eta_{x}\partial_{t}\mathring{K}=\alpha\Sigma\partial_{x}\mathring{K}-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathring{K}\eta_{x}\mathring{W}-\tfrac{1}{2}\eta_{x}\mathring{K}\mathring{Z}.

We can also derive a similar equation for
\mathring{Z}
. Since

\partial_{t}u+\lambda_{3}\partial_{y}u=\alpha\sigma\mathring{z},

we conclude that

\partial_{t}(u\!\circ\!\eta)=\alpha\Sigma\mathring{Z}.

Letting
\partial_{x}
act on both sides of this equation and rearranging shows that

\partial_{t}(\eta_{x}\mathring{Z})=2\alpha\partial_{x}(\Sigma\mathring{Z})-(\eta_{x}\mathring{W})_{t}.

Expanding this equation and using (
4.2b
) - (
4.2e
) gives us the identity

(4.4)

\eta_{x}\partial_{t}\mathring{Z}=2\alpha\Sigma\partial_{x}\mathring{Z}-\tfrac{1-\alpha}{2}\eta_{x}\mathring{W}\mathring{Z}-\tfrac{\alpha}{4\gamma}\Sigma\mathring{K}\eta_{x}\mathring{W}-\tfrac{1+\alpha}{2}\eta_{x}\mathring{Z}^{2}+\tfrac{\alpha}{4\gamma}\eta_{x}\Sigma\mathring{K}\mathring{Z}.

For the rest of the paper, we will work with the variables
\eta_{x},\Sigma,\eta_{x}\mathring{W},\mathring{Z},
and
\mathring{K}
, which solve the following system:

(4.5a)

\displaystyle\Sigma_{t}

\displaystyle=-\alpha\Sigma\mathring{Z}+\tfrac{\alpha}{2\gamma}\Sigma^{2}\mathring{K},

(4.5b)

\displaystyle\Sigma_{x}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\eta_{x}\mathring{W}-\tfrac{1}{2}\eta_{x}\mathring{Z}+\tfrac{1}{2\gamma}\eta_{x}\Sigma\mathring{K},

(4.5c)

\displaystyle(\Sigma^{-1})_{t}

\displaystyle=\alpha\Sigma^{-1}\mathring{Z}-\tfrac{\alpha}{2\gamma}\mathring{K},

(4.5d)

\displaystyle\eta_{xt}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1+\alpha}{2}\eta_{x}\mathring{W}+\tfrac{1-\alpha}{2}\eta_{x}\mathring{Z}+\tfrac{\alpha}{2\gamma}\eta_{x}\Sigma\mathring{K}

(4.5e)

\displaystyle(\eta_{x}\mathring{W})_{t}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{\alpha}{4\gamma}\Sigma\mathring{K}(\eta_{x}\mathring{W}+\eta_{x}\mathring{Z})

(4.5f)

\displaystyle\eta_{x}\partial_{t}\mathring{K}

\displaystyle=\alpha\Sigma\partial_{x}\mathring{K}-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathring{K}\eta_{x}\mathring{W}-\tfrac{1}{2}\eta_{x}\mathring{K}\mathring{Z}

(4.5g)

\displaystyle\eta_{x}\partial_{t}\mathring{Z}

\displaystyle=2\alpha\Sigma\partial_{x}\mathring{Z}-\tfrac{1-\alpha}{2}\eta_{x}\mathring{W}\mathring{Z}-\tfrac{\alpha}{4\gamma}\Sigma\mathring{K}\eta_{x}\mathring{W}-\tfrac{1+\alpha}{2}\eta_{x}\mathring{Z}^{2}+\tfrac{\alpha}{4\gamma}\eta_{x}\Sigma\mathring{K}\mathring{Z}.

Notice that we are treating the product
\eta_{x}\mathring{W}
as one of the basic variables rather than analyzing
\mathring{W}
on its own. This is because the product
\eta_{x}\mathring{W}
will remain as smooth as the initial data while
\mathring{W}
does not.

4.2.
Initial estimates and determination of the blowup time

We will show that
\Sigma,\Sigma^{-1},\eta_{x},\eta_{x}\mathring{W},\mathring{Z}
and
\mathring{K}
all remain bounded as long as the solution exists, that the solution exists up until a time
T_{*}=\frac{2}{1+\alpha}+\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}(\varepsilon)
, and that at time
T_{*}

\eta_{x}
must have a zero.

Proposition 4.1
.

Define the constants

(4.6)

B_{z}:=6^{\frac{2}{\min(1,\alpha)}}(2+\tfrac{1}{\gamma})e^{21},\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad B_{k}:=6^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}.

Note that
B_{k}<B_{z}
. If
\varepsilon
is chosen small enough such that

(1+B_{z})\varepsilon\ll 1,

then for all
(x,t)\in\mathbb{T}\times[0,T_{*}\wedge\tfrac{2}{1+\alpha}(1+\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}))
we have that

(4.7a)

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{3}\leq\Sigma

\displaystyle\leq 3,

(4.7b)

\displaystyle|\eta_{x}\mathring{W}|

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{4}{3},

(4.7c)

\displaystyle\eta_{x}

\displaystyle\leq 3,

(4.7d)

\displaystyle|\mathring{K}|

\displaystyle\leq B_{k}\varepsilon,

(4.7e)

\displaystyle|\mathring{Z}|

\displaystyle\leq B_{z}\varepsilon,

and

(4.8)

\displaystyle\eta_{x}\mathring{W}

\displaystyle\leq-\tfrac{1}{2}+4\eta_{x}.

Proof.

We will proceed with a bootstrap argument. Fix
T\in[0,T_{*}\wedge\tfrac{2}{1+\alpha}(1+\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}))
and pick a constant

B_{z}<A<2B_{z}.

We make the following bootstrap assumptions:

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{4}\leq\Sigma

\displaystyle\leq 4

\displaystyle\forall\>(x,t)\in\mathbb{T}\times[0,T],

\displaystyle|\eta_{x}\mathring{W}|

\displaystyle\leq 2

\displaystyle\forall\>(x,t)\in\mathbb{T}\times[0,T],

\displaystyle\eta_{x}

\displaystyle\leq 4

\displaystyle\forall\>(x,t)\in\mathbb{T}\times[0,T],

\displaystyle|\mathring{K}|

\displaystyle\leq A\varepsilon

\displaystyle\forall\>(x,t)\in\mathbb{T}\times[0,T],

\displaystyle|\mathring{Z}|

\displaystyle\leq A\varepsilon

\displaystyle\forall\>(x,t)\in\mathbb{T}\times[0,T].

It follows from (
4.2a
) that

\Sigma(x,t)=\sigma_{0}(x)\exp\bigg(\int^{t}_{0}(-\alpha\mathring{Z}+\tfrac{\alpha}{2\gamma}\Sigma\mathring{K})(x,s)\>ds\bigg).

Since
T\leq\frac{2}{1+\alpha}(1+\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}})
and
\frac{2\alpha}{1+\alpha}(1+\frac{2}{\gamma})<2
, it follows that

\Sigma,\Sigma^{-1}\leq 2e^{\alpha tA(1+\frac{2}{\gamma})\varepsilon}\leq 2e^{2A(1+\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}})\varepsilon}\,.

It follows that if
\varepsilon\ll B_{z}
and
\varepsilon\ll 1
then

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{3}\leq\Sigma

\displaystyle\leq 3

\displaystyle\forall\>(x,t)\in\mathbb{T}\times[0,T].

To improve upon the second bootstrap assumption, plugging
\Sigma\leq 3
and our bootstrap assumptions into (
4.2e
) gives us the bound

\displaystyle|(\eta_{x}\mathring{W})_{t}|

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{3}{8}A\varepsilon(2+4A\varepsilon).

Since

(\eta_{x}\mathring{W})(x,t)=w^{\prime}_{0}(x)-\tfrac{1}{2\gamma}\sigma_{0}(x)k^{\prime}_{0}(x)+\int^{t}_{0}(\eta_{x}\mathring{W})_{t}(x,s)\>ds

it follows that

(4.9)

\displaystyle|(\eta_{x}\mathring{W})(x,t)-w^{\prime}_{0}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{1}{\gamma}\varepsilon+\tfrac{2}{1+\alpha}(1+\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}})\tfrac{3}{8}A\varepsilon(2+4A\varepsilon)\,.

Therefore,

|\eta_{x}\mathring{W}|\leq 1+\varepsilon\bigl[1+\tfrac{1}{\gamma}+(1+\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}})\tfrac{3}{4}A(2+4A\varepsilon)\bigr]\leq\tfrac{4}{3}

if
B_{z}\varepsilon\ll 1
and
\varepsilon\ll 1
. This proves our second inequality.

To prove the third inequality in (
4.7
), our identity (
4.2d
) for
\eta_{xt}
paired with (
4.9
) gives us

\displaystyle\eta_{x}=1+\int^{t}_{0}\eta_{xt}\>ds

\displaystyle=1+\tfrac{1+\alpha}{2}tw^{\prime}_{0}+\tfrac{1+\alpha}{2}\int^{t}_{0}(\eta_{x}\mathring{W}-w^{\prime}_{0})\>ds+\tfrac{1-\alpha}{2}\int^{t}_{0}\eta_{x}\mathring{Z}\>ds+\tfrac{\alpha}{2\gamma}\int^{t}_{0}\eta_{x}\Sigma\mathring{K}\>ds

\displaystyle=1+\tfrac{1+\alpha}{2}tw^{\prime}_{0}+\mathcal{O}(\tfrac{1+\alpha}{2}t(1+A\varepsilon)(1+A)\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=1+\tfrac{1+\alpha}{2}tw^{\prime}_{0}+\mathcal{O}((1+A\varepsilon)(1+A)\varepsilon)

provided that
\varepsilon\ll 1
. Therefore

(4.10)

\displaystyle|\eta_{xt}(x,t)-1-\tfrac{1+\alpha}{2}tw^{\prime}_{0}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{O}((1+A\varepsilon)(1+A)\varepsilon)\,.

From here it follows that

\eta_{x}\leq 1+(1+\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}})(1+\varepsilon)+\mathcal{O}((1+A\varepsilon)(1+A)\varepsilon)\leq 3

for all
(x,t)\in\mathbb{T}\times[0,T]
.

Before improving upon the last two bootstrap assumptions, we will prove (
4.8
) for
t\leq T
. When
0\leq t\leq T\wedge\frac{1}{1+\alpha}
, (
4.10
) gives us

\displaystyle\eta_{x}

\displaystyle\geq 1+\tfrac{1+\alpha}{2}tw^{\prime}_{0}+\mathcal{O}((1+A\varepsilon)(1+A)\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\geq 1-\tfrac{1}{2}(1+\varepsilon)+\mathcal{O}((1+A\varepsilon)(1+A)\varepsilon).

Using this lower bound in conjunction with (
4.9
) results in

\displaystyle\eta_{x}\mathring{W}

\displaystyle\leq 1+\mathcal{O}((1+A\varepsilon)(1+A)\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=1-4\eta_{x}+4\eta_{x}+\mathcal{O}((1+A\varepsilon)(1+A)\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\leq-1+4\eta_{x}+\mathcal{O}((1+A\varepsilon)(1+A)\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\leq-\tfrac{1}{2}+4\eta_{x}.

The last inequality here is true provided that
(1+B_{z})\varepsilon\ll 1
. For
\frac{1}{1+\alpha}\leq t\leq T
we have
1+\alpha\geq\frac{1}{t}\geq\frac{1+\alpha}{2(1+\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}})}
, so the inequalities (
4.9
) and (
4.10
) imply that

\displaystyle\eta_{x}\mathring{W}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{2}{1+\alpha}\tfrac{1}{t}(\eta_{x}-1)+\tfrac{2}{1+\alpha}\tfrac{1}{t}(1+\tfrac{1+\alpha}{2}tw^{\prime}_{0}-\eta_{x})+(\eta_{x}\mathring{W}-w^{\prime}_{0})

\displaystyle\leq 2\eta_{x}-\tfrac{1}{1+\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}}+\tfrac{2}{1+\alpha}\tfrac{1}{t}(1+\tfrac{1+\alpha}{2}tw^{\prime}_{0}-\eta_{x})+(\eta_{x}\mathring{W}-w^{\prime}_{0})

\displaystyle=2\eta_{x}-\tfrac{1}{1+\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}}+\mathcal{O}((1+A\varepsilon)(1+A)\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\leq 2\eta_{x}-\tfrac{1}{2}.

This proves (
4.8
) for
(x,t)\in\mathbb{T}\times[0,T]
. We will now use this inequality to prove the last two inequalities.

For
1<q<\infty
and a constant
\delta\geq 0
to be determined, define the quantities

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{0,q}_{k}(t)

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Sigma^{-\delta q}\eta_{x}|\mathring{K}|^{q}\>dx,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{0,q}_{z}(t)

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Sigma^{-\delta q}\eta_{x}|\mathring{Z}|^{q}\>dx.

Using (
4.5
) we compute that

\displaystyle\dot{\mathcal{E}}^{0,q}_{k}

\displaystyle=-\delta q\int\tfrac{\Sigma_{t}}{\Sigma}\Sigma^{-\delta q}\eta_{x}|\mathring{K}|^{q}+\int\Sigma^{-\delta q}\eta_{xt}|\mathring{K}|^{q}+q\int\Sigma^{-\delta q}|\mathring{K}|^{q-1}\mathrm{sgn\,}(\mathring{K})\eta_{x}\partial_{t}\mathring{K}

\displaystyle=\int\Sigma^{-\delta q}\eta_{x}|\mathring{K}|^{q}[\alpha\delta q(\mathring{Z}-\tfrac{1}{2\gamma}\Sigma\mathring{K})+(\tfrac{1-\alpha}{2}\mathring{Z}+\tfrac{\alpha}{2\gamma}\Sigma\mathring{K})]

\displaystyle\quad+\int\Sigma^{-\delta q}|\mathring{K}|^{q}\tfrac{1+\alpha}{2}\eta_{x}\mathring{W}+q\int\Sigma^{-\delta q}|\mathring{K}|^{q-1}\mathrm{sgn\,}(\mathring{K})\eta_{x}\partial_{t}\mathring{K}

\displaystyle=\int\Sigma^{-\delta q}\eta_{x}|\mathring{K}|^{q}[\alpha\delta q(\mathring{Z}-\tfrac{1}{2\gamma}\Sigma\mathring{K})+(\tfrac{1-\alpha}{2}\mathring{Z}+\tfrac{\alpha}{2\gamma}\Sigma\mathring{K})]+\int\Sigma^{-\delta q}|\mathring{K}|^{q}\tfrac{1+\alpha}{2}\eta_{x}\mathring{W}

\displaystyle\quad+\alpha\int\Sigma^{1-\delta q}\partial_{x}\big(|\mathring{K}|^{q}\big)+\int\Sigma^{-\delta q}|\mathring{K}|^{q}[-\tfrac{q}{2}\eta_{x}\mathring{W}]+\int\Sigma^{-\delta q}\eta_{x}|\mathring{K}|^{q}[-\tfrac{q}{2}\mathring{Z}]

\displaystyle=\int\Sigma^{-\delta q}\eta_{x}|\mathring{K}|^{q}[\alpha\delta q(\mathring{Z}-\tfrac{1}{2\gamma}\Sigma\mathring{K})+(\tfrac{1-\alpha}{2}\mathring{Z}+\tfrac{\alpha}{2\gamma}\Sigma\mathring{K})]

\displaystyle\quad+\int\Sigma^{-\delta q}|\mathring{K}|^{q}\tfrac{1+\alpha}{2}\eta_{x}\mathring{W}+\int\Sigma^{-\delta q}|\mathring{K}|^{q}[\tfrac{1}{2}\alpha(\delta q-1)\eta_{x}\mathring{W}]

\displaystyle\quad+\alpha(\delta q-1)\int\Sigma^{-\delta q}\eta_{x}|\mathring{K}|^{q}[-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathring{Z}+\tfrac{1}{2\gamma}\Sigma\mathring{K}]+\int\Sigma^{-\delta q}|\mathring{K}|^{q}[-\tfrac{q}{2}\eta_{x}\mathring{W}]+\int\Sigma^{-\delta q}\eta_{x}|\mathring{K}|^{q}[-\tfrac{q}{2}\mathring{Z}]

\displaystyle=[\tfrac{1+\alpha}{2}+\tfrac{\alpha}{2}(\delta q-1)-\tfrac{q}{2}]\int\Sigma^{-\delta q}|\mathring{K}|^{q}\eta_{x}\mathring{W}

\displaystyle\quad+[\alpha\delta q+\tfrac{1-\alpha}{2}-\tfrac{\alpha}{2}(\delta q-1)-\tfrac{q}{2}]\int\Sigma^{-\delta q}\eta_{x}|\mathring{K}|^{q}\mathring{Z}+[-\alpha\delta q+\alpha+\alpha(\delta q-1)]\int\Sigma^{-\delta q}\eta_{x}|\mathring{K}|^{q}\tfrac{1}{2\gamma}\Sigma\mathring{K}

\displaystyle=[\tfrac{\alpha}{2}\delta q-\tfrac{q-1}{2}]\int\Sigma^{-\delta q}|\mathring{K}|^{q}\eta_{x}\mathring{W}+[\tfrac{\alpha}{2}\delta q-\tfrac{q-1}{2}]\int\Sigma^{-\delta q}\eta_{x}|\mathring{K}|^{q}\mathring{Z}.

Performing analogous computations for
\mathcal{E}^{0,q}_{z}
, we find that

(4.11a)

\displaystyle\dot{\mathcal{E}}^{0,q}_{k}

\displaystyle=[\tfrac{\alpha}{2}\delta q-\tfrac{q-1}{2}]\int\Sigma^{-\delta q}|\mathring{K}|^{q}\eta_{x}\mathring{W}+[\tfrac{\alpha}{2}\delta q-\tfrac{q-1}{2}]\int\Sigma^{-\delta q}\eta_{x}|\mathring{K}|^{q}\mathring{Z},

\displaystyle\dot{\mathcal{E}}^{0,q}_{z}

\displaystyle=[\alpha\delta q-\tfrac{1-\alpha}{2}(q-1)]\int\Sigma^{-\delta q}|\mathring{Z}|^{q}\eta_{x}\mathring{W}-q\tfrac{\alpha}{4\gamma}\int\Sigma^{-\delta q}|\mathring{Z}|^{q-1}\mathrm{sgn\,}(\mathring{Z})\Sigma\mathring{K}\eta_{x}\mathring{W}

(4.11b)

\displaystyle\quad-\tfrac{1+\alpha}{2}(q-1)\int\Sigma^{-\delta q}\eta_{x}|\mathring{Z}|^{q}\mathring{Z}+[\alpha\delta q+\alpha(\tfrac{q}{2}-1)]\int\Sigma^{-\delta q}\eta_{x}|\mathring{Z}|^{q}\tfrac{1}{2\gamma}\Sigma\mathring{K}.

First, let us derive bounds on
\mathring{K}
. Choosing
\delta=\frac{1}{\alpha}
and using (
4.11a
), (
4.8
), and our bound
\eta_{x}\leq 3
gives us

\dot{\mathcal{E}}^{0,q}_{k}=\tfrac{1}{2}\int\Sigma^{-\frac{q}{\alpha}}|\mathring{K}|^{q}\eta_{x}\mathring{W}+\tfrac{1}{2}\int\Sigma^{-\frac{q}{\alpha}}\eta_{x}|\mathring{K}|^{q}\mathring{Z}\leq-\tfrac{1}{4}\int\Sigma^{-\frac{q}{\alpha}}|\mathring{K}|^{q}+(2+\tfrac{A}{2}\varepsilon)\mathcal{E}^{0,q}_{k}\leq(\tfrac{23}{12}+\tfrac{A}{2}\varepsilon)\mathcal{E}^{0,q}_{k}.

and thus

\mathcal{E}^{0,q}_{k}(t)^{1/q}\leq\mathcal{E}^{0,q}_{k}(0)^{1/q}e^{\frac{1}{q}(\frac{23}{12}+\frac{A}{2}\varepsilon)t}\leq 2^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}\|k^{\prime}_{0}\|_{L^{q}_{x}}e^{\frac{1}{q}(\frac{23}{12}+\frac{A}{2}\varepsilon)t}.

Therefore, for any
t\in[0,T]
we have that

\|\Sigma^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}}\mathring{K}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}=\lim_{q\rightarrow\infty}\mathcal{E}^{0,q}_{k}(t)^{1/q}\leq\limsup_{q\rightarrow\infty}2^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}\|k^{\prime}_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}e^{\frac{1}{q}(\frac{23}{12}+\frac{A}{2}\varepsilon)t}=2^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}\|k^{\prime}_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}.

So

|\mathring{K}|\leq 6^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}\|k^{\prime}_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}},\qquad\forall\>(x,t)\in\mathbb{T}\times[0,T].

To bound
\mathring{Z}
, it is best to bound
\mathcal{E}^{0,q}_{k}+\mathcal{E}^{0,q}_{z}
. If
\delta
is large enough that
\tfrac{\alpha}{2}\delta q-\tfrac{q-1}{2}>0
and
\alpha\delta q-\tfrac{1-\alpha}{2}(q-1)>0
, then adding (
4.11a
) and (
4.11b
) together and using (
4.8
) gives us

\displaystyle\dot{\mathcal{E}}^{0,q}_{k}+\dot{\mathcal{E}}^{0,q}_{z}+\tfrac{1}{2}[\tfrac{\alpha}{2}\delta q-\tfrac{q-1}{2}]\int\Sigma^{-\delta q}|\mathring{K}|^{q}+\tfrac{1}{2}[\alpha\delta q-\tfrac{1-\alpha}{2}(q-1)]\int\Sigma^{-\delta q}|\mathring{Z}|^{q}

\displaystyle\leq[\tfrac{\alpha}{2}\delta q-\tfrac{q-1}{2}](4+A\varepsilon)\mathcal{E}^{0,q}_{k}+\big(4[\alpha\delta q-\tfrac{1-\alpha}{2}(q-1)]+\tfrac{3}{2\gamma}A\varepsilon[\alpha\delta q+\alpha(\tfrac{q}{2}-1)]+\tfrac{1+\alpha}{2}(q-1)A\varepsilon\big)\mathcal{E}^{0,q}_{z}

\displaystyle\quad-q\tfrac{\alpha}{4\gamma}\int\Sigma^{-\delta q}|\mathring{Z}|^{q-1}\mathrm{sgn\,}(\mathring{Z})\Sigma\mathring{K}\eta_{x}\mathring{W}.

Since
|\Sigma\eta_{x}\mathring{W}|\leq 3\cdot\frac{4}{3}=4
we have

\displaystyle-q\tfrac{\alpha}{4\gamma}\int\Sigma^{-\delta q}|\mathring{Z}|^{q-1}\mathrm{sgn\,}(\mathring{Z})\Sigma\mathring{K}\eta_{x}\mathring{W}

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{\alpha}{\gamma}(q-1)\int\Sigma^{-\delta q}|\mathring{Z}|^{q}+\tfrac{\alpha}{\gamma}\int\Sigma^{-\delta q}|\mathring{K}|^{q},

and our bound on
\dot{\mathcal{E}}^{0,q}_{k}+\dot{\mathcal{E}}^{0,q}_{z}
becomes

\displaystyle\dot{\mathcal{E}}^{0,q}_{k}+\dot{\mathcal{E}}^{0,q}_{z}+\tfrac{1}{2}[\tfrac{\alpha}{2}\delta q-\tfrac{q-1}{2}-\tfrac{2\alpha}{\gamma}]\int\Sigma^{-\delta q}|\mathring{K}|^{q}+\tfrac{1}{2}[\alpha\delta q-\tfrac{1-\alpha}{2}(q-1)-\tfrac{2\alpha}{\gamma}(q-1)]\int\Sigma^{-\delta q}|\mathring{Z}|^{q}

\displaystyle\leq[\tfrac{\alpha}{2}\delta q-\tfrac{q-1}{2}](4+A\varepsilon)\mathcal{E}^{0,q}_{k}+\big(4[\alpha\delta q-\tfrac{1-\alpha}{2}(q-1)]+\tfrac{3}{2\gamma}A\varepsilon[\alpha\delta q+\alpha(\tfrac{q}{2}-1)]+\tfrac{1+\alpha}{2}(q-1)A\varepsilon\big)\mathcal{E}^{0,q}_{z}.

Now choose
\delta=\frac{2}{\min(1,\alpha)}
. This choice of
\delta
is large enough that the factors in front of the damping terms are positive, and we get

\displaystyle\dot{\mathcal{E}}^{0,q}_{k}+\dot{\mathcal{E}}^{0,q}_{z}

\displaystyle\leq 10\max(1,\alpha)q[1+\tfrac{1}{2}A\varepsilon](\mathcal{E}^{0,q}_{k}+\mathcal{E}^{0,q}_{z}).

Since
T\leq\frac{2}{1+\alpha}(1+\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}})
, we conclude that
