\displaystyle|(\mathring{x},\mathring{T})(w^{1}_{0},z^{1}_{0},k^{1}_{0})-(\mathring{x},\mathring{T})(w^{2}_{0},z^{2}_{0},k^{2}_{0})|

\displaystyle\leq 405B_{d}\big[\tfrac{\overline{C}^{2n-1}_{x}}{(2n)^{2}}+\tfrac{2}{1+\alpha}\big]\|(w^{1}_{0}-w^{2}_{0},z^{1}_{0}-z^{2}_{0},k^{1}_{0}-k^{2}_{0})\|_{2n-1,C_{0}}.

It now also follows from Proposition
8.8
that
f_{n}
is a Lipschitz function of
(w_{0},z_{0},k_{0})
as well.

8.4.
An implicit function theorem argument

In this subsection, we will further restrict our attention to the case
n\geq 2
. Define the subspace

(8.23)

X_{n}:=\{\widetilde{w}_{0}\in W^{2n+2,\infty}(\mathbb{T}):\partial^{i}_{x}\widetilde{w}_{0}(0)=0\>\forall\>i=2,\ldots,2n-1\}.

Note that
\overline{w}_{0}\in X_{n}
.

Pick functions
\widetilde{w}^{n}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{w}^{n}_{2n-2}\in C^{\infty}_{x}(\mathbb{T})
such that

(8.24a)

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{w}^{n}_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}

\displaystyle\leq L_{n},

(8.24b)

\displaystyle\partial^{i+1}_{x}\widetilde{w}^{n}_{j}(0)

\displaystyle=\delta_{ij},

\displaystyle i=0,\ldots,2n-2,

(8.24c)

\displaystyle\frac{\|\partial^{i+1}_{x}\widetilde{w}^{n}_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}}{i!}

\displaystyle\leq L_{n}C^{i}_{0},

\displaystyle i=0,\ldots,2n+1,

(8.24d)

\displaystyle|\partial^{2n}_{x}\widetilde{w}^{n}_{j}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq(2n-1)!L_{n}(\tfrac{C_{0}}{2})^{2n-1}\varepsilon,

\displaystyle|x-0|\leq\tfrac{1}{C_{0}},

for some constant
L_{n}
. For such functions, Taylor expanding about
x=0
for
x
with
|x-0|\leq\frac{1}{C_{0}}
gives us

\displaystyle\bigg|\partial^{i+1}_{x}\widetilde{w}^{n}_{j}(x)-\mathbbm{1}_{\{i\leq j\}}\tfrac{x^{j-i}}{(j-i)!}\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{(2n-1)!L_{n}(\tfrac{C_{0}}{2})^{2n-1}\varepsilon}{(2n-1-i)!}|x|^{2n-1-i}

\displaystyle\leq i!L_{n}{2n-1\choose{i}}\tfrac{1}{2^{2n-1}}C^{i}_{0}\varepsilon

\displaystyle\leq i!L_{n}C^{i}_{0}\varepsilon

for
i=0,\ldots,2n
.

For a concrete example of such a collection of functions, pick a smooth bump function
\chi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R})
satisfying

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}_{[-1,1]}\leq\chi\leq\mathbbm{1}_{[-\frac{C_{0}}{2},\frac{C_{0}}{2}]}

and then define the
\mathbb{Z}
-periodic functions
\widetilde{w}_{j}
via

\displaystyle\widetilde{w}_{j}(x)

\displaystyle:=\tfrac{x^{j+1}}{(j+1)!}\chi(C_{0}x)

\displaystyle\text{ for }|x|\leq\tfrac{1}{2}.

One can check that for any
n\geq 2
if we define the functions
\widetilde{w}^{n}_{1},\ldots\widetilde{w}^{n}_{2n-2}
to be
\widetilde{w}^{n}_{j}:=\widetilde{w}_{j}
, then these functions satisfy (
8.24
) with

L_{n}=C_{0}(2n+2)\max_{0\leq k\leq 2n+2}\tfrac{\|\partial^{k}\chi\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}}{k!}.

Note, however, that in this example
L_{n}
must diverge to
\infty
as
n
grows.

Since
W^{2n+2,\infty}(\mathbb{T})=\mathbb{R}\widetilde{w}^{n}_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathbb{R}\widetilde{w}^{n}_{2n-2}\oplus X_{n}
and
\overline{w}_{0}\in X_{n}
, we have the affine change of coordinates
X_{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{2n-2}\longleftrightarrow W^{2n+2,\infty}(\mathbb{T})

\displaystyle(\widetilde{w}_{0},\lambda)

\displaystyle\longleftrightarrow w_{0},

\displaystyle w_{0}

\displaystyle=\overline{w}_{0}+\widetilde{w}_{0}+\lambda_{1}\widetilde{w}^{n}_{1}+\ldots+\lambda_{2n-2}\widetilde{w}^{n}_{2n-2},

\displaystyle\lambda_{j}

\displaystyle=\partial^{j+1}_{x}w_{0}(0).

This extends to an affine isomorphism
X_{n}\times(W^{2n+2,\infty}(\mathbb{T}))^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2n-2}\longleftrightarrow(W^{2n+2,\infty}(\mathbb{T}))^{3}

\displaystyle(\widetilde{w}_{0},z_{0},k_{0},\lambda)

\displaystyle\longleftrightarrow(w_{0},z_{0},k_{0}),

\displaystyle w_{0}

\displaystyle=\overline{w}_{0}+\widetilde{w}_{0}+\lambda_{1}\widetilde{w}^{n}_{1}+\ldots+\lambda_{2n-2}\widetilde{w}^{n}_{2n-2},

\displaystyle\lambda_{j}

\displaystyle=\partial^{j+1}_{x}w_{0}(0).

This change of coordinates for
(W^{2n+2,\infty}(\mathbb{T}))^{3}
will allow us to characterize the zero set of
f_{n}
as a codimension-
(2n-2)
Banach manifold in a neighborhood of the point
(\overline{w}_{0},0,0)\in(W^{2n+2,\infty}(\mathbb{T}))^{3}
.

With this choice of coordinates in mind, define the open set

(8.25)

\displaystyle\Lambda_{n}(\varepsilon,L_{n})

\displaystyle:=\big\{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{2n-2}:L_{n}\sum^{2n-2}_{j=1}|\lambda_{j}|<\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}\big\},

and define the open set

U_{n}(\varepsilon,C_{0})\subset X_{n}\times(W^{2n+2,\infty}(\mathbb{T}))^{2}

to be
(\widetilde{w}_{0},z_{0},k_{0})\in X_{n}\times(W^{2n+2,\infty}(\mathbb{T}))^{2}
satisfying

(8.26a)

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{w}_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}

\displaystyle<\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2},

(8.26b)

\displaystyle\|z_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}

\displaystyle<\varepsilon,

(8.26c)

\displaystyle\frac{\|\partial^{i+1}_{x}\widetilde{w}_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}}{i!}

\displaystyle<C^{i}_{0}\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2},

\displaystyle i=0,\ldots,2n+1,

(8.26d)

\displaystyle\frac{\|\partial^{i+1}_{x}z_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}}{i!}

\displaystyle<C^{i}_{0}\varepsilon,

\displaystyle i=0,\ldots,2n+1,

(8.26e)

\displaystyle\frac{\|\partial^{i+1}_{x}k_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}}{i!}

\displaystyle<C^{i}_{0}\varepsilon,

\displaystyle i=0,\ldots,2n+1.

Note that if
(\widetilde{w}_{0},z_{0},k_{0},\lambda)\in U_{n}(\varepsilon,C_{0})\times\Lambda_{n}(\varepsilon,L_{n})
and
w_{0}=\overline{w}_{0}+\widetilde{w}_{0}+\lambda_{1}\widetilde{w}^{n}_{1}+\ldots+\lambda_{2n-2}\widetilde{w}^{n}_{2n-2}
, then
(w_{0},z_{0},k_{0})\in\mathcal{B}_{n}(\varepsilon,C_{0})
. For this reason, we can treat the functions
\mathring{x},\mathring{T},
and
f_{n}
defined in §
8.2
as functions of
(w_{0},z_{0},k_{0})\in\mathcal{B}_{n}(\varepsilon,C_{0})
as functions of
(\widetilde{w}_{0},z_{0},k_{0},\lambda)\in U_{n}(\varepsilon,C_{0})\times\Lambda_{n}(\varepsilon,L_{n})
by identifying
(\widetilde{w}_{0},z_{0},k_{0},\lambda)
with the corresponding
(w_{0},z_{0},k_{0})\in\mathcal{B}_{n}(\varepsilon,C_{0})
via the affine change of coordinates. We will make this tacit identification for the rest of this subsection.

We will further assume for the rest of this section that
\overline{C}_{t},\overline{C}_{x}
and
\varepsilon
are chosen to satisfy the hypotheses of the propositions in §
6

with
L=L_{n},M=5L_{n}
. Therefore, for all solutions with initial data
(\widetilde{w}_{0},z_{0},k_{0},\lambda)\in U_{n}(\varepsilon,C_{0})\times\Lambda_{n}(\varepsilon,L_{n})
, the partial derivatives
\mathring{Z}_{\lambda_{j}},\partial_{\lambda_{j}}\eta_{x},
etc. exist for
j=1,\ldots,2n-2
and the solution satisfies the estimates from §
6.4
.

It follows from (
8.21
) that if
j\in\{1,\ldots,2n-2\}
,
(w_{0},z_{0},k_{0})\in\mathcal{B}_{n}(\varepsilon,C_{0})
, and
(w_{0}+\Delta\lambda_{j}\widetilde{w}^{n}_{j},z_{0},k_{0})\in\mathcal{B}_{n}(\varepsilon,C_{0})
for some scalar
\Delta\lambda_{j}
we have

\displaystyle|T_{*}(w_{0}+\Delta\lambda_{j}\widetilde{w}^{n}_{j},z_{0},k_{0})-T_{*}(w_{0},z_{0},k_{0})|

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{140}{1+\alpha}L_{n}B_{d}\Delta\lambda_{j}.

Therefore, for each
(\widetilde{w}_{0},z_{0},k_{0})\in U_{n}(\varepsilon,C_{0})
the map
\lambda\in\Lambda_{n}(\varepsilon,C_{0})\rightarrow T_{*}(\widetilde{w}_{0},z_{0},k_{0},\lambda)
is
\frac{140}{1+\alpha}L_{n}B_{d}
-Lipschitz in each
\lambda_{j}
. It follows that the map
\lambda\rightarrow T_{*}(\widetilde{w}_{0},z_{0},k_{0},\lambda)
is a
(2n-2)^{\frac{1}{2}}\frac{140}{1+\alpha}L_{n}B_{d}
-Lipschitz map of
\lambda\in\Lambda_{n}(\varepsilon,C_{0})
for each
(\widetilde{w}_{0},z_{0},k_{0})\in U_{n}(\varepsilon,C_{0})
and therefore is differentiable at almost every
\lambda\in\Lambda_{n}(\varepsilon,C_{0})
with

(8.27a)

\displaystyle\|\partial_{\lambda_{j}}T_{*}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\lambda}(\Lambda_{n}(\varepsilon,L_{n}))}

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{140}{1+\alpha}L_{n}B_{d}

\displaystyle\forall\>j=1,\ldots,2n-2.

Since
B_{\lambda}>4B_{d}
, (
8.27a
) implies

(8.27b)

\displaystyle\|\partial_{\lambda_{j}}T_{*}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\lambda}(\Lambda_{n}(\varepsilon,L_{n}))}

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{35}{1+\alpha}L_{n}B_{\lambda}

\displaystyle\forall\>j=1,\ldots,2n-2.

Lemma 8.9
.

For all
(\widetilde{w}_{0},z_{0},k_{0})\in U_{n}(\varepsilon,C_{0})
,
i=0,\ldots,2n-1
,
j=1,\ldots,2n-2
, and
(x,t)\in\mathbb{T}\times[0,\infty)
we have that

(8.28a)

\displaystyle\|\partial^{i}_{x}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}\widetilde{\eta}_{x}(x,t)-\tfrac{1+\alpha}{2}t\partial^{i+1}_{x}\widetilde{w}^{n}_{j}(x)\|_{L^{\infty}_{\lambda}(\Lambda_{n}(\varepsilon,C_{0}))}

\displaystyle\lesssim(1+\alpha)ti!L_{n}\overline{C}^{i}_{x}\overline{C}_{t}B_{\lambda}B_{z}\varepsilon,

and for values of
x\in\mathbb{T}
with
|x-0|\leq\frac{1}{C_{0}}
we have that

(8.28b)

\displaystyle\|\partial^{i}_{x}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}\widetilde{\eta}_{x}(x,t)-\tfrac{1+\alpha}{2}t\mathbbm{1}_{\{i\leq j\}}\tfrac{x^{j-i}}{(j-i)!}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\lambda}(\Lambda_{n}(\varepsilon,C_{0}))}

\displaystyle\lesssim(1+\alpha)ti!L_{n}\overline{C}^{i}_{x}\overline{C}_{t}B_{\lambda}B_{z}\varepsilon.

It follows that

(8.28c)

\displaystyle\|\partial^{i}_{x}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}\widetilde{\eta}_{x}(\mathring{x},\mathring{T})-\tfrac{1+\alpha}{2}\mathring{T}\delta_{ij}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\lambda}(\Lambda_{n}(\varepsilon,C_{0}))}

\displaystyle\lesssim\tfrac{1+\alpha}{2}\mathring{T}\big(\tfrac{(2n)!\overline{C}^{2n-1}_{x}}{(2n)^{3}}+i!L_{n}\overline{C}^{i}_{x}\overline{C}_{t}B_{\lambda}\big)B_{z}\varepsilon.

Proof.

The inequality (
8.28a
) is an application of Corollary
6.8
and (
8.27b
). The bound (
8.28b
) follows from (
8.28a
) and the identity

\displaystyle\partial^{i+1}_{x}\widetilde{w}^{n}_{j}(x)

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{1}_{\{i\leq j\}}\tfrac{x^{j-i}}{(j-i)!}+\mathcal{O}\big(i!L_{n}C^{i}_{0}\varepsilon\big)

\displaystyle|x-0|\leq\tfrac{1}{C_{0}},i=0,\ldots,2n-1

derived earlier in this section. The last inequality (
8.28c
) follows from plugging
(x,t)=(\mathring{x},\mathring{T})
into (
8.28b
) and applying (
8.17
).
∎

Proposition 8.10
.

Let
n\geq 2
and let
f_{n}
be the function defined in (
8.19
). If

(2n)!L_{n}\overline{C}^{2n-1}_{x}\overline{C}_{t}B_{\lambda}B_{z}\varepsilon\ll 1

then there exists a function
\lambda_{*}:U_{n}(\varepsilon^{2},C_{0})\rightarrow\Lambda_{n}(\varepsilon,C_{0})
, Lipschitz with respect to the
(W^{2n+2,\infty}(\mathbb{T}))^{3}
norm, such that

\bigg\{(\widetilde{w}_{0},z_{0},k_{0},\lambda)\in U_{n}(\varepsilon^{2},C_{0})\times\Lambda_{n}(\varepsilon,C_{0}):f_{n}=0\bigg\}=\bigg\{(\widetilde{w}_{0},z_{0},k_{0},\lambda_{*}(\widetilde{w}_{0},z_{0},k_{0})):(\widetilde{w}_{0},z_{0},k_{0})\in U_{n}(\varepsilon,C_{0})\bigg\}.

Proof.

First, we apply
\partial_{\lambda_{j}}
to the equation
G(\mathring{x},\mathring{T})=(0,0)
and get

\begin{bmatrix}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}\mathring{x}\\
\partial_{\lambda_{j}}\mathring{T}\end{bmatrix}=-DG(\mathring{x},\mathring{T})^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}\tfrac{1}{(2n)!}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}\partial^{2n-1}_{x}\widetilde{\eta}_{x}(\mathring{x},\mathring{T})\\
-\tfrac{2}{1+\alpha}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}\eta_{x}(\mathring{x},\mathring{T})\end{bmatrix}.

We know from the proof of Proposition
8.6
that
\|\mathrm{Id\,}-DG(\mathring{x},\mathring{T})\|_{\text{operator}}\leq|\mathrm{Id\,}-DG(\mathring{x},\mathring{T})|\leq\frac{2}{3}
, so it follows (use Neumann series) that
\|DG(\mathring{x},\mathring{T})^{-1}\|_{\text{operator}}\leq 3
. It therefore follows from (
8.28c
) that

\displaystyle\|\partial_{\lambda_{j}}\mathring{x}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\lambda}(\Lambda_{n}(\varepsilon,C_{0}))}

\displaystyle\lesssim\tfrac{1+\alpha}{2}\mathring{T}\tfrac{1}{2n}\big(1+L_{n}\overline{C}_{t}B_{\lambda}\big)\overline{C}^{2n-1}_{x}B_{z}\varepsilon,

\displaystyle\|\partial_{\lambda_{j}}\mathring{T}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\lambda}(\Lambda_{n}(\varepsilon,C_{0}))}

\displaystyle\lesssim\tfrac{1+\alpha}{2}\mathring{T}\tfrac{1}{2n}\big(1+L_{n}\overline{C}_{t}B_{\lambda}\big)\overline{C}^{2n-1}_{x}B_{z}\varepsilon.

Using these bounds for
\partial_{\lambda_{j}}\mathring{x}
and
\partial_{\lambda_{j}}\mathring{T}
in conjunction with the bounds (
8.18a
), (
8.18b
), and (
8.28c
), we compute that for
i,j=1,\ldots,2n-2
we have that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial f^{i}_{n}}{\partial\lambda_{j}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda_{j}}\big[\partial^{i}_{x}\widetilde{\eta}_{x}(\mathring{x},\mathring{T})\big]

\displaystyle=\partial^{i}_{x}\partial_{\lambda_{j}}\widetilde{\eta}_{x}(\mathring{x},\mathring{T})+\partial^{i+1}_{x}\widetilde{\eta}_{x}(\mathring{x},\mathring{T})\partial_{\lambda_{j}}\mathring{x}+\partial^{i}_{x}\widetilde{\eta}_{xt}(\mathring{x},\mathring{T})\partial_{\lambda_{j}}\mathring{T}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1+\alpha}{2}\mathring{T}[\delta_{ij}+\tfrac{1}{(2n)^{3}}\mathcal{O}((2n)!\overline{C}^{2n-1}_{x}B_{z}\varepsilon)+\mathcal{O}(i!L_{n}\overline{C}^{i}_{x}\overline{C}_{t}B_{\lambda}B_{z}\varepsilon)]

\displaystyle\quad+\tfrac{1+\alpha}{2}\mathring{T}\tfrac{1}{2n}\mathcal{O}((2n)!\overline{C}^{2n-1}_{x}B_{z}\varepsilon)\mathcal{O}\big(\tfrac{1}{2n}\big(1+L_{n}\overline{C}_{t}B_{\lambda}\big)\overline{C}^{2n-1}_{x}B_{z}\varepsilon\big).

It follows that if
(2n)!L_{n}\overline{C}^{2n-1}_{x}\overline{C}_{t}B_{\lambda}B_{z}\varepsilon
is small enough we have that

\displaystyle\|\partial_{\lambda_{j}}f^{i}_{n}-\tfrac{1+\alpha}{2}\mathring{T}\delta_{ij}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\lambda}(\Lambda_{n}(\varepsilon,C_{0}))}\leq\tfrac{1}{19}\tfrac{1+\alpha}{2}\mathring{T}\tfrac{1}{(2n)^{2}}

for all
i,j=1,\ldots,2n-2
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\big\|D_{\lambda}f_{n}-\mathrm{Id\,}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\lambda}(\Lambda_{n}(\varepsilon,C_{0}))}\leq\tfrac{1}{9}.

If
(\widetilde{w}_{0},z_{0},k_{0})\in U_{n}(\varepsilon^{2},C_{0})
and
\lambda=0
, then
(w_{0},z_{0},k_{0})=(\widetilde{w}_{0},z_{0},k_{0})\in U_{n}(\varepsilon^{2},C_{0})\subset\mathcal{B}_{n}(\varepsilon^{2},C_{0})
. It follows from (
8.18b
) that

\displaystyle|f^{i}_{n}(\widetilde{w}_{0},z_{0},k_{0},0)|

\displaystyle\leq 50\frac{(2n)!L_{n}\overline{C}^{2n-1}_{x}B_{z}\varepsilon}{(2n)}\frac{\varepsilon}{L_{n}}.

for
i=1,\ldots,2n-2
,
(\widetilde{w}_{0},z_{0},k_{0})\in U_{n}(\varepsilon^{2},C_{0})
. Therefore, if
(2n)!L_{n}\overline{C}^{2n-1}_{x}B_{z}\varepsilon
is small enough we have

\displaystyle|f_{n}(\widetilde{w}_{0},z_{0},k_{0},0)|

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{\varepsilon}{10L_{n}}

\displaystyle\forall\>(\widetilde{w}_{0},z_{0},k_{0})\in U_{n}(\varepsilon^{2},C_{0}).

It now follows (see §
C
) that we can apply the implicit function theorem to
f_{n}
and conclude our result.
∎

Another way of phrasing Proposition
8.10
is as follows: if we define the open neighborhood

(8.29)

\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{n}(\varepsilon,C_{0},L_{n}):=\{(\overline{w}_{0}+\sum^{2n-2}_{j=1}\lambda_{j}\widetilde{w}^{n}_{j}+\widetilde{w}_{0},z_{0},k_{0}):(\widetilde{w}_{0},z_{0},k_{0},\lambda)\in U_{n}(\varepsilon^{2},C_{0})\times\Lambda_{n}(\varepsilon,L_{n})\big\}

of
(\overline{w}_{0},0,0)
in
(W^{2n+2,\infty}(\mathbb{T}))^{3}
, then the zero set of
f_{n}
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{n}(\varepsilon,C_{0})
is a codimension-
(2n-2)
,
C^{0,1}
Banach manifold.

9.
A detailed description of the cusp structure at the first singularity

We will now prove our main theorem, which will imply Theorem
1.1
.

Theorem 9.1

(
Finite-codimension stable shock formation).

Fix an integer
n\geq 1
, a value of
\alpha=\frac{\gamma-1}{2}>0
, and a constant
C_{0}\geq 3
. Define
\overline{w}_{0}
as in (
8.1
). Then there exists a codimension-
(2n-2)
Banach manifold
11
11

11

In the case
n=1
,
\mathcal{M}_{1}
is simply an open subset of
(W^{4,\infty}(\mathbb{T}))^{3}
. When
n\geq 2
, it is the graph of a Lipschitz function.

\mathcal{M}_{n}\subset(W^{2n+2,\infty}(\mathbb{T}))^{3}
containing
(\overline{w}_{0},0,0)
and a constant

\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}(n,\alpha,C_{0})

such that if
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
then for all initial data
(w_{0},z_{0},k_{0})\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
satisfying

(9.1)

\displaystyle\|w_{0}-\overline{w}_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}

\displaystyle<\varepsilon,

\displaystyle\|z_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}

\displaystyle<\varepsilon,

\displaystyle\frac{\|\partial^{i+1}_{x}(w_{0}-\overline{w}_{0})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}}{i!}

\displaystyle<C^{i}_{0}\varepsilon,

\displaystyle\forall\>i=0,\ldots,2n+1,

\displaystyle\frac{\|\partial^{i+1}_{x}z_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}}{i!}

\displaystyle<C^{i}_{0}\varepsilon,

\displaystyle\forall\>i=0,\ldots,2n+1,

\displaystyle\frac{\|\partial^{i+1}_{x}k_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}}{i!}

\displaystyle<C^{i}_{0}\varepsilon

\displaystyle\forall\>i=0,\ldots,2n+1,

the unique locally-well posed solution
(w,z,k)
to (
3.2
) with initial data
(w_{0},z_{0},k_{0})
satisfies the following:

(i)

(w,z,k)
exists as a classical solution up until a finite time
T_{*}
with

(9.2)

T_{*}=\tfrac{2}{1+\alpha}[1+\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}(\varepsilon)];

(ii)

the functions
z
and
k
remain uniformly
C^{1,\frac{1}{2n+1}}
on
\mathbb{T}\times[0,T_{*}]
with

(9.3)

\displaystyle[\partial_{y}z]_{C^{0,\frac{1}{2n+1}}_{y}}\lesssim_{n,\alpha,C_{0}}\varepsilon,\qquad[\partial_{y}k]_{C^{0,\frac{1}{2n+1}}_{y}}\lesssim_{n,\alpha,C_{0}}\varepsilon,

but
w
has a gradient blowup at a unique point
(y_{*},T_{*})\in\mathbb{T}\times[0,T_{*}]
with
y_{*}
satisfying

(9.4)

|y_{*}-\tfrac{1}{2}|\lesssim_{n,\alpha,C_{0}}\varepsilon;

(iii)

away from
(y_{*},T_{*})
,
w,z,
and
k
remain locally
C^{2n+1,1}
in
\mathbb{T}\times[0,T_{*}]
;

(iv)

for all
y
in the interval
|y-y_{*}|<\tfrac{1}{(2n+2)(2n+1)2^{2n+2}C^{2n+1}_{0}}
we have

(9.5a)

\displaystyle w(y,T_{*})

\displaystyle=b_{0}+b_{1}(y-y_{*})^{\frac{1}{2n+1}}+\mathcal{O}_{C_{0}}(|y-y_{*}|^{\frac{2}{2n+1}}),

(9.5b)

\displaystyle\partial_{y}w(y,T_{*})

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2n+1}b_{1}(y-y_{*})^{-\frac{2n}{2n+1}}+\mathcal{O}_{n,\alpha,C_{0}}(|y-y_{*}|^{-\frac{2n-1}{2n+1}}),

where
b_{0}
and
b_{1}
are constants satisfying

\displaystyle b_{0}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{5}{2}+\mathcal{O}_{n,\alpha,C_{0}}(\varepsilon),

\displaystyle b_{1}

\displaystyle=-(2n+1)^{\frac{1}{2n+1}}[1+\mathcal{O}_{n,\alpha,C_{0}}(\varepsilon)].

Proof.

Recall that the constants
B_{k},B_{z},B_{d},B_{\lambda}
are determined by
\alpha
via the definitions

\displaystyle B_{k}

\displaystyle:=6^{\frac{1}{\alpha}},

\displaystyle B_{z}

\displaystyle:=6^{\frac{2}{\min(1,\alpha)}}(2+\tfrac{1}{\gamma})e^{21},

\displaystyle B_{d}

\displaystyle:=2\cdot 6^{\frac{3}{\min(1,\alpha)}}e^{31},

\displaystyle B_{\lambda}

\displaystyle:=\tfrac{5}{2}\cdot 9^{\frac{3}{\min(1,\alpha)}}\cdot e^{31}.

Choose

\delta:=\tfrac{6}{\min(1,\alpha)}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\kappa:=\max(1,\alpha)(2+5\delta)

so that
\delta
and
\kappa
satisfy (
5.10
). With this choice of
\delta
and
\kappa
, choose the positive constant
C_{t}(0)
to satisfy

\displaystyle C_{t}(0)\gg(1+\alpha)3^{\delta},\qquad C_{t}(0)\gg\max(1,\alpha)2^{\delta}C_{0},

where the implicit constants here are those given by the hypotheses of Propositions
5.4
,
6.5
, and
7.1
. Now define

\displaystyle\overline{C}_{t}

\displaystyle:=C_{t}(0)e^{(11+\log 3)\delta+5}

in accordance with definition (
5.27
), and pick a constant
\overline{C}_{x}
large enough that

\displaystyle\overline{C}_{x}\gg 1,\qquad\alpha\overline{C}_{x}\gg\overline{C}_{t}+1,\qquad\overline{C}_{x}\gg(1+\alpha)C_{0},

where the implicit constants are sufficient to satisfy the hypotheses on
\overline{C}_{x}
in §
5
–
7
and Proposition
8.6
.

Now choose
\varepsilon_{0}>0
small enough that

\displaystyle(1+\alpha)^{2}B^{2}_{z}\varepsilon_{0}

\displaystyle\ll 1,

\displaystyle 3^{\frac{3}{\min(1,\alpha)}}B_{d}(\overline{C}_{t}+1+\alpha)B_{z}\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\ll 1,

\displaystyle\overline{C}_{t}B_{\lambda}B_{z}\varepsilon

\displaystyle\ll 1,

\displaystyle\overline{C}^{2n}_{x}B_{z}\varepsilon_{0}

\displaystyle\ll 1,

so that
\mathcal{B}_{n}(\varepsilon,C_{0})\subset\mathcal{A}_{n}(\varepsilon,C_{0})
and the hypotheses of §
4
–
8.2
are satisfied for any choice of
\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
. If
n\geq 2
, pick a choice of functions
\widetilde{w}^{n}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{w}^{n}_{2n-2}
with a corresponding constant
L_{n}
as described in §
8.4
and add the constraint

\displaystyle(2n)!L_{n}\overline{C}^{2n-1}_{x}\overline{C}_{t}B_{\lambda}B_{z}\varepsilon_{0}

\displaystyle\ll 1,

so that the hypotheses of Proposition
8.10
are satisfied for any
\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
. Lastly, if
R_{n}
and
M_{n}
are the constants defined in §
A
, we will add the constraints that
\varepsilon_{0}
is small enough that

(9.6)

\displaystyle 93\overline{C}^{2n}_{x}B_{z}\varepsilon_{0}

\displaystyle<1,

\displaystyle\tfrac{22(1+\varepsilon_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}})}{(n+1)^{2}}(\tfrac{1}{C_{0}}+\tfrac{1}{6n^{3}\overline{C}_{x}})\overline{C}^{2n+1}_{x}B_{z}\varepsilon_{0}

\displaystyle<1,

\displaystyle\tfrac{88(1+\varepsilon_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}})}{(n+1)^{2}R_{n}}(\tfrac{1}{C_{0}}+\tfrac{1}{6n^{3}\overline{C}_{x}})\overline{C}^{2n+1}_{x}B_{z}\varepsilon_{0}

\displaystyle<1,

\displaystyle\tfrac{88(1+\varepsilon_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}})M_{n}}{(n+1)^{2}R_{n}}\overline{C}^{2n+1}_{x}B_{z}\varepsilon_{0}

\displaystyle<1,

\displaystyle\tfrac{44(1+\varepsilon_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}})}{(n+1)^{2}}\overline{C}^{2n+1}_{x}B_{z}\varepsilon_{0}

\displaystyle<1.

These last constraints (
9.6
) will be used in some of our computations below.

With the constants
\overline{C}_{x},\overline{C}_{t},L_{n}
, and
\varepsilon_{0}
now chosen, define
\mathcal{M}_{n}\subset(W^{2n+2,\infty}(\mathbb{T}))^{3}
to be

\mathcal{M}_{n}:=\begin{cases}\mathcal{B}_{1}(\varepsilon_{0},C_{0})&n=1\\
\{(w_{0},z_{0},k_{0})\in\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{n}(\varepsilon_{0},C_{0},L_{n}):f_{n}(w_{0},z_{0},k_{0})=0\}&n\geq 2\end{cases},

where
\mathcal{B}_{1}(\varepsilon_{0},C_{0})\subset\mathcal{A}_{1}(\varepsilon_{0},C_{0})
is the open set defined by (
8.2
) when
n=1
,
f_{n}
is the function defined by (
8.19
), and
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{n}(\varepsilon,C_{0},L_{n})
is the open set defined by (
8.29
). We know from Proposition
8.10
that when
n\geq 2
,
\mathcal{M}_{n}
is a codimension-
(2n-2)
Banach submanifold of
(W^{2n+2,\infty}(\mathbb{T}))^{3}
, i.e. it is the graph of a Lipschitz function from a codimension-
(2n-2)
linear subspace of
(W^{2n+2,\infty}(\mathbb{T}))^{3}
into
\mathbb{R}^{2n-2}
.

Fix
\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
and a choice of initial data
(w_{0},z_{0},k_{0})\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
satisfying (
9.1
). According to Proposition
4.2
, the blowup time
T_{*}
is finite and satisfies (
4.12a
)–(
4.12b
). Since

\partial_{t}(w\!\circ\!\eta)=\tfrac{\alpha}{2\gamma}\Sigma^{2}\mathring{K},\qquad\partial_{t}(z\!\circ\!\eta)=2\alpha\Sigma\mathring{Z}-\tfrac{\alpha}{2\gamma}\Sigma^{2}\mathring{K},\qquad\partial_{t}(k\!\circ\!\eta)=\alpha\Sigma\mathring{K},

it follows from (
4.7
) and our assumptions on
w_{0},z_{0},k_{0}
that

(9.7)

w\!\circ\!\eta=\overline{w}_{0}+\mathcal{O}(B_{k}\varepsilon),\qquad z\circ\eta=z_{0}+\mathcal{O}(\alpha B_{z}\varepsilon),\qquad k\circ\eta=k_{0}+\mathcal{O}(\alpha B_{k}\varepsilon).

We know (see the discussion at the end of §
8.2
) that because
(w_{0},z_{0},k_{0})\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
,
\eta_{x}
has a unique zero in
\mathbb{T}\times[0,T_{*}]
at a point
(x_{*},T_{*})
, that

(9.8)

\partial_{x}\eta_{x}(x_{*},T_{*})=\ldots=\partial^{2n-1}_{x}\eta_{x}(x_{*},T_{*})=0,

and that
x_{*}
satisfies the bounds
|x_{*}-0|<\frac{1}{3(1+\alpha)C_{0}}
and (
8.12
). (
8.17
) and (
9.6
) also imply that

(9.9)

|x_{*}|\leq\tfrac{93}{(2n)^{3}}\overline{C}^{2n-1}_{x}B_{z}\varepsilon<\tfrac{1}{(2n)^{3}\overline{C}_{x}}.

The estimates in §
5
prove that the map
\eta(\cdot,T_{*}):\mathbb{T}\rightarrow\mathbb{T}
is a
C^{2n+1,1}
homeomorphism. It follows from (
9.8
) that
\eta(\cdot,T_{*})
lifts to a map
x\rightarrow y
from
\mathbb{R}
to
\mathbb{R}
with a Taylor expansion

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle=y_{*}+a_{2n+1}(x-x_{*})^{2n+1}+a_{2n+2}(x)(x-x_{*})^{2n+2}.

In what follows, we will continue to abuse notation and use
x
to refer both to the Lagrangian variable
x\in\mathbb{T}
and to the lifted variable
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Additionally, we will abuse notation and use
y
to refer both to the Eulerian variable
y=\eta(x,T_{*})\in\mathbb{T}
and the lifted variable
y\in\mathbb{R}
. So, for example, when we show below that the zeroth order term
y_{*}\in\mathbb{R}
of the above Taylor expansion satisfies
y_{*}=\frac{5}{2}+\mathcal{O}_{n,\alpha,C_{0}}(\varepsilon)
we can conclude that the corresponding Eulerian variable
y_{*}=\eta(x_{*},T_{*})\in\mathbb{T}=\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
satisfies
|y_{*}-\frac{1}{2}|\lesssim_{n,\alpha,C_{0}}\varepsilon
.

Because

a_{2n+1}=\tfrac{\partial^{2n}_{x}\eta_{x}(x_{*},T_{*})}{(2n+1)!},

it follows from (
8.4b
), (
4.12b
), and (
8.10
) that

(9.10a)

\displaystyle a_{2n+1}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2n+1}[1+\mathcal{O}(\overline{C}^{2n}_{x}B_{z}\varepsilon)],

(9.10b)

\displaystyle a_{2n+1}

\displaystyle>\tfrac{1}{2(2n+1)}.

Using (
8.4b
) and (
4.12a
), we obtain

(9.11)

|a_{2n+2}(x)|\leq 44(1+\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}})\tfrac{\overline{C}^{2n+1}_{x}B_{z}\varepsilon}{(2n+2)^{3}},\qquad\forall\>|x|\leq\tfrac{1}{C_{0}}.

The inequalities (
9.9
),(
9.10b
), (
9.11
) together with the hypotheses (
9.6
) allow us to apply Lemma
A.3
with
r=\frac{1}{C_{0}}
and obtain

(9.12)

\displaystyle\bigg|(x-x_{*})-\big(\tfrac{y-y_{*}}{a_{2n+1}}\big)^{\frac{1}{2n+1}}\bigg|

\displaystyle<\big|\tfrac{y-y_{*}}{a_{2n+1}}\big|^{\frac{2}{2n+1}}

\displaystyle\forall\>|x|\leq\tfrac{1}{C_{0}}.

Lemma
A.3
and the fact that
\frac{1}{C_{0}}-|x_{*}|>\frac{1}{2C_{0}}
lets us conclude that
\{y:\eta(x,T_{*})=y,|x|\leq\frac{1}{C_{0}}\}
contains the ball

|y-y_{*}|<\tfrac{1}{(2n+2)(2n+1)2^{2n+2}C^{2n+1}_{0}}.

Taylor expanding
w\circ\eta(\cdot,T_{*})
in
x
about
x_{*}
gives us

w\!\circ\!\eta(x,T_{*})=w\!\circ\!\eta(x_{*},T_{*})+\partial_{x}(w\!\circ\!\eta)(x_{*},T_{*})(x-x_{*})+\mathcal{O}\big(\|\partial^{2}_{x}(w\!\circ\!\eta)(\cdot,T_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}(x-x_{*})^{2}\big).

(
9.7
), (
9.9
), and our hypotheses on
\overline{w}_{0}
imply that if we define
B^{w}_{0}:=w\!\circ\!\eta(x_{*},T_{*})
then

\displaystyle w\!\circ\!\eta(x_{*},T_{*})

\displaystyle=\tfrac{5}{2}+\mathcal{O}(\tfrac{\overline{C}^{2n-1}_{x}B_{z}\varepsilon}{(2n)^{3}})+\mathcal{O}(B_{k}\varepsilon).

Since

\displaystyle\partial_{x}(w\!\circ\!\eta)=w^{\prime}_{0}-\tfrac{1}{2\gamma}\sigma_{0}k^{\prime}_{0}+\int^{t}_{0}(\eta_{x}\mathring{W})_{t}\>ds+\tfrac{1}{2\gamma}\eta_{x}\Sigma\mathring{K},

our derivative estimates from §
5
tell us that

(9.13)

\displaystyle\frac{(i+1)^{2}\|\partial^{i+1}_{x}(w\!\circ\!\eta)-\partial^{i+1}_{x}\overline{w}_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}}{i!\overline{C}^{i}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim B_{k}\varepsilon

for
t\in[0,T_{*}]
,
i=0,\ldots,2n+1
. Therefore, if we define

(9.14)

B^{w}_{1}:=\partial_{x}(w\!\circ\!\eta)(x_{*},T_{*})=-1+x^{2n}_{*}+\partial_{x}(w\!\circ\!\eta)(x_{*},T_{*})-\overline{w}^{\prime}_{0}(x_{*})

then (
8.12a
) and (
9.13
) give us

(9.15)

B^{w}_{1}=-1+\mathcal{O}(B_{z}\varepsilon).

Our assumptions on
\overline{C}_{x}
and
\varepsilon_{0}
imply that
\overline{C}_{x}B_{z}\varepsilon_{0}<1
, so (
9.13
) also yields

(9.16)

\|\partial^{2}_{x}(w\!\circ\!\eta)(\cdot,T_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\leq C_{0}+\mathcal{O}(\overline{C}_{x}B_{k}\varepsilon)\lesssim C_{0}.

We thus arrive at the expansion

w\!\circ\!\eta(x,T_{*})=B^{w}_{0}+B^{w}_{1}(x-x_{*})+\mathcal{O}(C_{0}(x-x_{*})^{2})

for all
x
. Plugging (
9.12
) into this equation gives us

\displaystyle w(y,T_{*})

\displaystyle=B^{w}_{0}+B^{w}_{1}\big(\tfrac{y-y_{*}}{a_{2n+1}}\big)^{\frac{1}{2n+1}}[1+\mathcal{O}\big(\big|\tfrac{y-y_{*}}{a_{2n+1}}\big|^{\frac{1}{2n+1}}\big)]+\mathcal{O}\big(C_{0}\big|\tfrac{y-y_{*}}{a_{2n+1}}\big|^{\frac{2}{2n+1}}\big)

\displaystyle=B^{w}_{0}+B^{w}_{1}\big(\tfrac{y-y_{*}}{a_{2n+1}}\big)^{\frac{1}{2n+1}}+\mathcal{O}\big((1+C_{0})\big|\tfrac{y-y_{*}}{a_{2n+1}}\big|^{\frac{2}{2n+1}}\big)

for all
|y-y_{*}|<\frac{1}{(2n+2)(2n+1)2^{2n+2}C^{2n+1}_{0}}
. If we define

(9.17)

\begin{split}b_{0}&:=B^{w}_{0}=\tfrac{5}{2}+\mathcal{O}(\overline{C}^{2n}_{x}B_{z}\varepsilon),\\
b_{1}&:=B^{w}_{1}a^{-\frac{1}{2n+1}}_{2n+1}=-[1+\mathcal{O}(B_{z}\varepsilon)](\tfrac{1}{2n+1})^{-\frac{1}{2n+1}}[1+\mathcal{O}(\overline{C}^{2n}_{x}B_{z}\varepsilon)]^{-\frac{1}{2n+1}}\\
&=-(2n+1)^{\frac{1}{2n+1}}[1+\mathcal{O}(\overline{C}^{2n}_{x}B_{z}\varepsilon)],\end{split}

then, using (
9.10b
), we arrive at our expansion (
9.5a
) for
w(\cdot,T_{*})
.

Taylor expanding
\eta_{x}(\cdot,T_{*})
about
x_{*}
and using (
9.8
), (
8.4b
), (
4.12a
), and (
9.6
) gives us

(9.18a)

\displaystyle\eta_{x}(x,T_{*})

\displaystyle=(2n+1)a_{2n+1}(x-x_{*})^{2n}+h_{2n+1}(x)(x-x_{*})^{2n+1},

(9.18b)

\displaystyle|h_{2n+1}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{44(1+\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}})}{(2n+2)^{2}}\overline{C}^{2n+1}_{x}B_{z}\varepsilon<\tfrac{1}{4}

\displaystyle\forall\>|x|\leq\tfrac{1}{C_{0}}.

We know from (
9.16
) that

\displaystyle\partial_{x}(w\!\circ\!\eta)(x,T_{*})

\displaystyle=B^{w}_{1}+\mathcal{O}\big(\|\partial^{2}_{x}(w\!\circ\!\eta)(\cdot,T_{*})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}(x-x_{*})\big)

\displaystyle=B^{w}_{1}+\mathcal{O}\big(C_{0}(x-x_{*})\big)

for all
x
. Therefore, it follows from (
9.12
) that for all
y
in the interval
|y-y_{*}|<\frac{1}{(2n+2)(2n+1)2^{2n+2}C^{2n+1}_{0}}
we have that

\displaystyle\partial_{y}w(y,T_{*})

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial_{x}(w\!\circ\!\eta)(x,T_{*})}{\eta_{x}(x,T_{*})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\big[B^{w}_{1}+\mathcal{O}\big(C_{0}(x-x_{*})\big)\big]}{(2n+1)a_{2n+1}}(x-x_{*})^{-2n}\big[1+\mathcal{O}(\overline{C}^{2n+1}_{x}B_{z}\varepsilon(x-x_{*}))\big]

\displaystyle=\frac{B^{w}_{1}+\mathcal{O}(C_{0}|\frac{y-y_{*}}{a_{2n+1}}|)}{(2n+1)a_{2n+1}}\big(\tfrac{y-y_{*}}{a_{2n+1}}\big)^{-\frac{2n}{2n+1}}\big[1+\mathcal{O}\big(\overline{C}^{2n+1}_{x}B_{z}\varepsilon\big|\tfrac{y-y_{*}}{a_{2n+1}}\big|^{\frac{1}{2n+1}}\big)]

\displaystyle=-\tfrac{1}{2n+1}b_{1}(y-y_{*})^{-\frac{2n}{2n+1}}+\mathcal{O}_{n,\alpha,C_{0}}\big(\big|\tfrac{y-y_{*}}{a_{2n+1}}\big|^{-\frac{2n-1}{2n+1}}\big)

which gives us (
9.5b
).

To approximate the location of
y_{*}
, using (
4.12b
), (
9.7
), and our bounds on
|x_{*}|
gives us

y_{*}=\eta(x_{*},T_{*})=x_{*}+\int^{T_{*}}_{0}\lambda_{3}\!\circ\!\eta(x_{*},t)\>dt=\tfrac{5}{2}+\tfrac{\mathcal{O}(\overline{C}^{2n}_{x}B_{z}\varepsilon)}{n^{3}\overline{C}_{x}}+\mathcal{O}((1+\alpha)B_{z}\varepsilon).

This implies (
9.4
).

To prove
C^{1,\frac{1}{2n+1}}_{y}
estimates on
z
and
k
, we will first show that
\eta^{-1}(\cdot,t)
is uniformly
C^{0,\frac{1}{2n+1}}_{y}
up to time
T_{*}
. In particular, we will show that

(9.19)

\displaystyle|x_{1}-x_{2}|

\displaystyle\leq 2^{2-\frac{1}{2n+1}}C^{2n}_{0}|\eta(x_{1},t)-\eta(x_{2},t)|^{\frac{1}{2n+1}}

\displaystyle\forall\>x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{T},t\in[0,T_{*}].

We will prove (
9.19
) by bounding
|x_{1}-x_{2}|
separately on the segments
|x-0|\leq\frac{1}{C_{0}}
and
|x-0|\geq\frac{1}{C_{0}}
.

We know from Lemma
8.2
that

\eta_{x}\geq\tfrac{1}{2}C^{-2n}_{0}

for
|x-0|\geq\frac{1}{C_{0}}
. Therefore, for all
x_{1},x_{2}
in this segment we have the bound

\displaystyle|x_{1}-x_{2}|

\displaystyle\leq 2C^{2n}_{0}|\eta(x_{1},t)-\eta(x_{2},t)|\leq 2C^{2n}_{0}|\eta(x_{1},t)-\eta(x_{2},t)|^{\frac{1}{2n+1}}

\displaystyle\forall\>t\in[0,T_{*}].

Now consider
x_{1},x_{2}
with
-\frac{1}{C_{0}}\leq x_{1}<x_{2}<\frac{1}{C_{0}}
. We know from (
8.4a
) that
\eta_{xt}(x,t)<0
for all
|x-0|\leq\frac{1}{C_{0}},t\in[0,T_{*}]
, and therefore,

\displaystyle|\eta(x_{2},t)-\eta(x_{1},t)|=\int^{x_{2}}_{x_{1}}\eta_{x}(x,t)\>dx\geq\int^{x_{2}}_{x_{1}}\eta_{x}(x,T_{*})\>dx=|\eta(x_{2},T_{*})-\eta(x_{1},T_{*})|

for all
t\in[0,T_{*}]
. Therefore, it suffices to bound
|\eta(x_{2},T_{*})-\eta(x_{1},T_{*})|
below. In the case where
x_{*}\leq x_{1}
, (
9.18
) and (
9.10b
) imply

\displaystyle\eta(x_{2},T_{*})-\eta(x_{1},T_{*})

\displaystyle=\int^{x_{2}}_{x_{1}}\eta_{x}(x,T_{*})\>dx

\displaystyle\geq a_{2n+1}\int^{x_{2}}_{x_{1}}(2n+1)(x-x_{*})^{2n}\>dx-\tfrac{1}{4}\int^{x_{2}}_{x_{1}}(x-x_{*})^{2n+1}\>dx

\displaystyle=a_{2n+1}\big[(x_{2}-x_{*})^{2n+1}-(x_{1}-x_{*})^{2n+1}\big]-\tfrac{1}{4(2n+2)}\big[(x_{2}-x_{*})^{2n+2}-(x_{1}-x_{*})^{2n+2}\big]

\displaystyle\geq\big[a_{2n+1}-\tfrac{1}{4(2n+2)}\tfrac{2n+1}{2n+2}\big](x_{2}-x_{1})^{2n+1}

\displaystyle\geq\tfrac{1}{4(2n+1)}(x_{2}-x_{1})^{2n+1}.

The same argument works for the case where
x_{2}\leq x_{*}
. In the last case where
x_{1}<x_{*}<x_{2}
, (
9.12
) gives us the bound

\displaystyle|x_{2}-x_{1}|

\displaystyle\leq\big|\tfrac{y_{2}-y_{1}}{a_{2n+1}}\big|^{\frac{1}{2n+1}}+\big|\tfrac{y_{2}-y_{*}}{a_{2n+1}}\big|^{\frac{2}{2n+1}}+\big|\tfrac{y_{1}-y_{*}}{a_{2n+1}}\big|^{\frac{2}{2n+1}}

\displaystyle\leq 3\big|\tfrac{y_{2}-y_{1}}{a_{2n+1}}\big|^{\frac{1}{2n+1}}

\displaystyle\leq 3(2(2n+1))^{\frac{1}{2n+1}}|y_{2}-y_{1}|^{\frac{1}{2n+1}},

where
y_{i}=\eta(x_{i},T_{*})
. Therefore

|x_{2}-x_{1}|\leq 6|\eta(x_{2},t)-\eta(x_{1},t)|^{\frac{1}{2n+1}}

for all
x_{1},x_{2}
with
|x_{i}|\leq\frac{1}{C_{0}}
,
t\in[0,T_{*}]
. Putting this together with our bounds for
|x-0|\geq\tfrac{1}{C_{0}}
, we conclude that (
9.19
) holds.

It now follows from our estimates in §
5
that

|\partial_{y}k(y_{1},t)-\partial_{y}k(y_{2},t)|=|\mathring{K}(x_{1},t)-\mathring{K}(x_{2},t)|\leq\|\partial_{x}\mathring{K}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x,t}}|x_{1}-x_{2}|\leq C^{2n}_{0}\overline{C}_{x}B_{k}\varepsilon|y_{1}-y_{2}|^{\frac{1}{2n+1}}.

Analogous computations also give us uniform
C^{0,\frac{1}{2n+1}}_{y}
estimates on
\partial_{y}z
.

It is also straightforward consequence of our estimates from §
5
that
w\circ\eta,z\circ\eta,
and
k\circ\eta
are
C^{2n+1,1}_{x,t}
on
\mathbb{T}\times[0,T_{*}]
and the inverse map
(x,t)\rightarrow(y,t)
is locally
C^{2n+1,1}_{x,t}
away from
(x_{*},T_{*})
, so
w,z,
and
k
are locally
C^{2n+1,1}_{y,t}
on
\mathbb{T}\times[0,T_{*}]\setminus\{(y_{*},T_{*})\}
.
∎

Appendix A
Polynomial inversion

This section generalizes the results from the appendix of a previous work of the authors
[
39
]
. Let
\mathbb{C}((z))
denote the field of formal Laurent series in the variable
z
with coefficients in
\mathbb{C}
, i.e. the field of formal power series with coefficients in
\mathbb{C}
that also allow for finitely many terms of negative degree. The field of
Puiseux series
in the variable
x
with coefficients in
\mathbb{C}
is then defined to be the union
\bigcup_{j>0}\mathbb{C}((x^{1/j}))
, which is itself a field. The Puiseux-Newton theorem states that
\bigcup_{j>0}\mathbb{C}((x^{1/j}))
is in fact an algebraically closed field. We will now introduce a useful special case of the Puiseux-Newton theorem:

Theorem A.1

(Analytic Puiseux-Newton)
.

If
\mathbb{C}\{x\}
denotes the ring of convergent power series in
x
, and
f(x,y)\in\mathbb{C}\{x\}[y]
is a polynomial of degree
m>0
, irreducible in
\mathbb{C}\{x\}[y]
, then there exists a convergent power series
y\in\mathbb{C}\{z\}
such that the roots of
f
in
\bigcup_{j>0}\mathbb{C}((x^{1/j}))
are all given by

y(x^{1/m}),y(e^{2\pi i/m}x^{1/m}),\ldots,y(e^{2\pi i\tfrac{m-1}{m}}x^{1/m}).

It follows that in general if
f(x,y)\in\mathbb{C}\{x\}[y]
then for each Puiseux series solution
y
of
f(x,y(x))=0
there exists some
\overline{y}\in\mathbb{C}\{z\}
and
m\leq\deg f
such that
y(x)=\overline{y}(x^{1/m})
.

Proof of Theorem
A.1
.

See
[
4
, Section 8.3]
.
∎

Now, fix a positive integer
n
. Recursively define the sequence

\displaystyle c^{n}_{0}

\displaystyle:=1,

\displaystyle c^{n}_{m}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{2n+2}=m-1}c^{n}_{\ell_{1}}\cdots c^{n}_{\ell_{2n+2}}-\tfrac{1}{2n+1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}+\cdots j_{2n+1}=m\\
0\leq j_{i}\leq m-1\end{subarray}}c^{n}_{j_{1}}\cdots c^{n}_{j_{2n+1}},

and then define the formal power series

\overline{y}_{n}(x):=\sum^{\infty}_{j=0}\tfrac{(-1)^{j}}{(2n+1)^{j}}c_{j}x^{j}.

One can check that

y_{0}(x):=x^{\frac{1}{2n+1}}\overline{y}_{n}(x^{\frac{1}{2n+1}})

is a Puiseux series solution of the algebraic equation

-x+y_{0}^{2n+1}+y^{2n+2}_{0}=0.

It follows from Theorem
A.1
that
\overline{y}_{n}
is a convergent power series with some positive radius of convergence

R_{n}:=\frac{2n+1}{\limsup_{j\rightarrow\infty}|c^{n}_{j}|^{1/j}}>0.

If
a_{2n+1}\in\mathbb{R}^{\times},a_{2n+2}\in\mathbb{R}
one can check that

\displaystyle y(x)

\displaystyle:=(\tfrac{x}{a_{2n+1}})^{\frac{1}{2n+1}}\overline{y}_{n}\big(\tfrac{a_{2n+2}}{a_{2n+1}}(\tfrac{x}{a_{2n+1}})^{\frac{1}{2n+1}}\big)

\displaystyle=(\tfrac{x}{a_{2n+1}})^{\frac{1}{2n+1}}\sum^{\infty}_{j=0}\big(\tfrac{-a_{2n+2}}{(2n+1)a_{2n+1}}\big)^{j}c^{n}_{j}(\tfrac{x}{a_{2n+1}})^{\frac{j}{2n+1}}

solves

-x+a_{2n+1}y^{2n+1}+a_{2n+2}y^{2n+2}=0

for all
x
satisfying

|a_{2n+2}|^{2n+1}|x|<a_{2n+1}^{2n+2}R_{n}^{2n+1}.

Since
\overline{y}_{n}
is holomorphic, if
0<r<R_{n}
then Cauchy’s estimate gives us

\displaystyle|\tfrac{c_{j}}{(2n+1)^{j}}|

\displaystyle\leq r^{-j}\max_{|z|=r|}|\overline{y}_{n}(z)|

for all nonnegative integers
j
. It follows that for
0<r<R_{n}
and
N\geq 0
, we have

\displaystyle\big|\overline{y}_{n}(z)-\sum^{N}_{j=0}\tfrac{(-1)^{j}}{(2n+1)^{j}}c_{j}z^{j}\big|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{|\tfrac{z}{r}|^{N+1}}{1-|\tfrac{z}{r}|}\max_{|z|=r}|\overline{y}_{n}(z)|

\displaystyle\forall\>|z|<r.

Therefore, if we define

(A.1)

M_{n}:=\max_{|z|=\frac{3R_{n}}{4}}|\overline{y}_{n}(z)|,

then for any
N\geq 0
we get the bound

(A.2)

\displaystyle\big|y(x)-(\tfrac{x}{a_{2n+1}})^{\frac{1}{2n+1}}\sum^{N}_{j=0}\big(\tfrac{-a_{2n+2}}{(2n+1)a_{2n+1}}\big)^{j}c^{n}_{j}(\tfrac{x}{a_{2n+1}})^{\frac{j}{2n+1}}\big|

\displaystyle\leq 3M_{n}(\tfrac{4a_{2n+2}}{3a_{2n+1}R_{n}})^{N+1}|\tfrac{x}{a_{2n+1}}|^{\frac{N+2}{2n+1}}

\displaystyle\forall\>x\text{ s.t. }\>|a_{2n+2}|^{2n+1}|x|

\displaystyle<a_{2n+1}^{2n+2}(\tfrac{R_{n}}{2})^{2n+1}.

Lemma A.2
.

Let
n
be a positive integer, let
I\subset\mathbb{R}
be an interval, and let
y\in C^{2n+1,1}(I)
. Suppose
y
has a Taylor series expansion about
x_{*}\in I
of the form

y(x)=y_{*}+a_{2n+1}(x-x_{*})^{2n+1}+a_{2n+2}(x)(x-x_{*})^{2n+2}

where
a_{2n+1}>0
. Define
J\subset I
to be

J:=\{x\in I:|a_{2n+2}(x)|^{2n+1}|y(x)-y_{*}|<a_{2n+1}^{2n+2}R^{2n+1}_{n},\quad\tfrac{(2n+2)a_{2n+2}(x)(x-x_{*})}{(2n+1)a_{2n+1}}>-1\},

and define
\widetilde{J}
to be the connected component of
J
containing
x_{*}
. Then for all
x\in\widetilde{J}
we have

(A.3)

(x-x_{*})=\big(\tfrac{y(x)-y_{*}}{a_{2n+1}}\big)^{\frac{1}{2n+1}}\sum^{\infty}_{j=0}\big(\tfrac{-a_{2n+2(x)}}{(2n+1)a_{2n+1}}\big)^{j}c^{n}_{j}\big(\tfrac{y(x)-y_{*}}{a_{2n+1}}\big)^{\frac{j}{2n+1}}.

Proof.

Without loss of generality,
x_{*}=y_{*}=0
. For
(y,a)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
satisfying

|a|^{2n+1}|y|<a_{2n+1}^{2n+2}R^{2n+1}_{n}

define the function
\widetilde{x}
via the series

\widetilde{x}(y,a):=\big(\tfrac{y}{a_{2n+1}}\big)^{\frac{1}{2n+1}}\sum^{\infty}_{j=0}\big(\tfrac{-a}{(2n+1)a_{2n+1}}\big)^{j}c^{n}_{j}\big(\tfrac{y}{a_{2n+1}}\big)^{\frac{j}{2n+1}}

which we know to converge. For
(y,a)
in the domain of
\widetilde{x}
,
\widetilde{x}(y,a)
solves

-y+a_{2n+1}\widetilde{x}(y,a)^{2n+1}+a\widetilde{x}(y,a)^{2n+2}=0.

For all
x\in J
,
(y(x),a_{2n+2}(x))
is in the domain of
\widetilde{x}
and we have

(A.4)

\displaystyle y(x)

\displaystyle=a_{2n+1}\widetilde{x}(y(x),a_{2n+2}(x)))^{2n+1}+a_{2n+2}(x)\widetilde{x}(y(x),a_{2n+2}(x))^{2n+2}=0.

For
x\in\widetilde{J}
, define the bootstrap hypothesis and conclusion

\displaystyle H(x)

\displaystyle:=``\tfrac{(2n+2)a_{2n+2}(x)\widetilde{x}(y(x),a_{2n+2}(x))}{(2n+1)a_{2n+1}}>-1"

\displaystyle C(x)

\displaystyle:=``\widetilde{x}(y(x),a_{2n+2}(x))=x."

We note that

(i)

H(0)
is true,

(ii)

the set of
x\in\widetilde{J}
where
C(x)
is true is a closed subset of
\widetilde{J}
because
x\rightarrow\widetilde{x}(y(x),a_{2n+2}(x))
is continuous, and

(iii)

the set of
x\in\widetilde{J}
where
C(x)
is true is in the interior of the set where
H(x)
is true, by virtue of the definition of
J
and the fact that
x\rightarrow\widetilde{x}(y(x),a_{2n+2}(x))
is continuous.

Lastly, the set where
H(x)
is true contains the set where
C(x)
is true. To see this, for
(x,z)\in I\times\mathbb{R}
satisfying

\tfrac{(2n+2)a_{2n+2}(x)z}{(2n+1)|a_{2n+1}|}>-1

define the function

f(x,z):=a_{2n+1}z^{2n+1}+a_{2n+2}(x)z^{2n+2}.

For all
(x,z)
in the domain of
f
we have

\displaystyle\partial_{z}f(x,z)

\displaystyle=(2n+1)a_{2n+1}z^{2n}[1+\tfrac{(2n+2)a_{2n+2}(x)z}{(2n+1)a_{2n+1}}]

so that
f
is a strictly increasing function of
z
everywhere in its domain. If
x\in\widetilde{J}
is such that
H(x)
is true, then
\widetilde{x}(y(x),a_{2n+2}(x))
is in the domain of
f
and (
A.4
) implies that

f(x,\widetilde{x}(y(x),a_{2n+2}(x)))=y(x)=f(x,x).

Since
f
is a strictly increasing function of its second argument on its domain, we conclude that
C(x)
is true. This completes our bootstrap argument, and since
\widetilde{J}
is connected we conclude that
C(x)
is true for all
x\in\widetilde{J}
.
∎

Lemma A.3
.

In the context of the previous lemma, if we further assume that
I=[-r,r]
and
r
satisfies

(A.5a)

\displaystyle\tfrac{(2n+2)\|a_{2n+2}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}}{(2n+1)a_{2n+1}}(r+|x_{*}|)

\displaystyle<1,

(A.5b)

\displaystyle\tfrac{\|a_{2n+2}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}}{a_{2n+1}R_{n}}(r+|x_{*}|)

\displaystyle<\tfrac{1}{4},

then for all
x\in I
we have

(A.6)

\bigg|(x-x_{*})-\big(\tfrac{y(x)-y_{*}}{a_{2n+1}}\big)^{\frac{1}{2n+1}}\bigg|\leq\tfrac{4M_{n}\|a_{2n+2}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}}{a_{2n+1}R_{n}}|\tfrac{y(x)-y_{*}}{a_{2n+1}}|^{\frac{2}{2n+1}}.

Furthermore, the range of
y
contains the ball

(A.7)

\displaystyle|y-y_{*}|

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{a_{2n+1}(r-|x_{*}|)^{2n+1}}{2n+2}.

Proof.

It is immediate from (
A.5a
) that

|y(x)-y_{*}|\geq\tfrac{a_{2n+1}}{2n+2}|x-x_{*}|^{2n+1}

for all
x\in I
. This implies that the range of
y
contains the ball defined by (
A.7
). Furthermore, (
A.5
) implies that
J=I
and therefore
\widetilde{J}=I
. Lemma
A.2
therefore implies that (
A.3
) holds for all
x\in I
. Now our assumption (
A.5b
) allows us to apply (
A.2
) with
N=1
to obtain (
A.6
).
∎

Appendix B
Frequently used computations

The following lemmas are straightforward exercises:

Lemma B.1
.

If
A>0
and
f\in\mathbb{R}
are constants, and
x:[0,T]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
satisfies

\dot{x}\leq-Ax+f

for all
t\in[0,T]
, then

\displaystyle x(t)

\displaystyle\leq x(0)e^{-At}+(1-e^{-At})\tfrac{f}{A}\leq\max(x(0),\tfrac{f}{A})

\displaystyle\forall\>t\in[0,T].

Lemma B.2
.

If
\beta
is a multi-index and
j_{1}+\ldots+j_{m}=|\beta|
, then

(B.1)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma_{1}+\cdots+\gamma_{m}=\beta\\
|\gamma_{i}|=j_{i}\end{subarray}}{\beta\choose{\gamma_{1}\cdots\gamma_{m}}}={|\beta|\choose{j_{1}\cdots j_{m}}}.

Lemma B.3
.

For all positive integers
m,\ell
and all
p>1
, we have the bound

\displaystyle\sum_{j_{1}+\ldots+j_{\ell}=m}\tfrac{(m+1)^{p}}{(j_{1}+1)^{p}\cdots(j_{\ell}+1)^{p}}

\displaystyle<\ell^{1+p}\bigg(\sum^{\infty}_{j=1}\tfrac{1}{j^{p}}\bigg)^{\ell-1}.

In particular, for
p=2
and
\ell=2,3
we have

(B.2)

\displaystyle\sum^{m}_{j=0}\tfrac{(m+1)^{2}}{(j+1)^{2}(m+1-j)^{2}}<\tfrac{4\pi^{2}}{3},

\displaystyle\sum_{j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}=m}\tfrac{(m+1)^{2}}{(j_{1}+1)^{2}(j_{2}+1)^{2}(j_{3}+1)^{2}}<\tfrac{3\pi^{4}}{4}.

Many variants of the inequalities (
B.2
) can be proven via similar computations:

(B.3)

\displaystyle\sum^{m}_{j=0}\tfrac{m+2}{(j+1)^{2}(m+2-j)}<\tfrac{2\pi^{2}}{3},

\displaystyle\sum^{m}_{j=1}\tfrac{m+2}{j^{3}(m+2-j)}<8,

\displaystyle\sum^{m}_{j=2}\tfrac{m+1}{j^{3}(m+2-j)}<6.1,

\displaystyle\sum^{m}_{j=1}\tfrac{(m+1)^{2}}{j^{3}(m+1-j)^{2}}<25,

\displaystyle\sum^{m-1}_{j=0}\tfrac{(m+1)^{2}}{(j+1)^{2}(m-j)^{2}}<\tfrac{16\pi^{2}}{3},

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}=m\\
j_{1}\geq 1\end{subarray}}\tfrac{(m+1)^{2}}{j_{1}^{3}(j_{2}+1)^{2}(j_{3}+1)^{2}}<163

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}=m\\
j_{1}\geq 1\end{subarray}}\tfrac{m+2}{j_{1}^{3}(j_{2}+1)^{2}(j_{3}+2)}<21,

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}=m\\
j_{1},j_{2}\geq 1\end{subarray}}\tfrac{(m+1)^{2}}{j_{1}^{3}j^{3}_{2}(j_{3}+1)^{2}}<66,

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}+j_{4}=m\\
j_{1},j_{2}\geq 1\end{subarray}}\tfrac{(m+1)^{2}}{j^{3}_{1}j^{3}_{2}(j_{3}+1)^{2}(j_{4}+1)^{2}}<641.

These inequalities (
B.2
)–(
B.3
) are used throughout §
5
-
6
. Note that the righthand side of the inequalities (
B.2
)–(
B.3
) is always
independent of
m
.

Appendix C
Implicit function theorem

In this section, suppose that
X
is a Banach space,
U\subset X
is an open set, and
V\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
is an open, convex set with
B_{r}(y_{0})\subseteq V\subseteq B_{R}(y_{0})
for some
y_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{N},0<r\leq R
. Let
f:U\times V\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{N}
.

Lemma C.1

(Existence of unique solutions)
.

Suppose that
f
is Lipschitz in
y
and that there exists a constant
\theta\in[0,1)
such that for all
x\in U
we have

(C.1a)

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{Id\,}-Df_{y}(x,\cdot)\|_{L^{\infty}_{y}(V)}

\displaystyle\leq\theta,

(C.1b)

\displaystyle|f(x,y_{0})|

\displaystyle\leq r-\theta R.

Then there exists a function
g:U\rightarrow V
such that

\displaystyle\bigg\{(x,y)\in U\times V:f(x,y)=0\bigg\}=\bigg\{(x,g(x)):x\in U\bigg\}.

Proof.

For each
x\in U
, define the map
\Psi(x):V\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{N}
,
\Psi(x)(y):=y-f(x,y)
. Since
V
is convex, it follows from (
C.1a
) that
\Psi(x)
is
\theta
-Lipschitz for all
x\in U
. Therefore, (
C.1b
) implies that

\displaystyle\Psi(x)(V)\subset B_{\theta R}(\Psi(x)(y_{0}))\subset B_{r}(y_{0})\subset V.

Thus
\Psi(x)
is a contraction mapping for each
x\in U
. Define
g(x)
to be the unique fixed point of
\Psi(x)
.
∎

Lemma C.2

(Lipschitz implicit function theorem)
.

In the context of the previous lemma, if we additionally assume that
f
is uniformly Lipschitz in
x
with

(C.2)

\displaystyle|f(x_{1},y)-f(x_{2},y)|

\displaystyle\leq L|x_{1}-x_{2}|

\displaystyle\forall\>x_{1},x_{2}\in U,y\in V,

then the function
g
is
\frac{L}{1-\theta}
-Lipschitz.

Proof.

Recursively define the sequence of functions

\begin{cases}g_{0}(x):=x\\
g_{n+1}(x):=\Psi(x)(g_{n}(x))\end{cases}.

It is immediate that
[g_{n+1}]_{C^{0,1}_{x}(U)}\leq\theta[g_{n}]_{C^{0,1}_{x}(U)}+L
for all
n
. Therefore, we have

[g_{n+1}]_{C^{0,1}_{x}(U)}\leq L\sum^{n}_{j=0}\theta^{j}<\frac{L}{1-\theta}

for all
n
. Since
g_{n}
converges pointwise to
g
, our result follows.
∎
